2.1.0 - 2026-09-28
------------------
**Variables keep their values across timepoints, as the LRM's static variables do; and the
memristor-crossbar spiking neuron of Xuan et al. (IJCNN 2026) reproduced, where the gap was found.**

  * **The gap was a silent wrong answer.** A variable assigned only inside an event body
    (`@(cross(...)) n = n + 1;`, `@(initial_step) s = 3;`, a flip-flop's stored bit) had no
    binding at the timepoints between events. Its read failed, `load` stopped its statement walk
    there (by design), and **every contribution after the read went unstamped**, with no
    diagnostic: a counter never counted, a flip-flop never held. Build-time validation walks both
    arms of the `if` an event desugars to, so it saw the assignment and passed the model.
  * **The fix (`va-codegen` only, no interface change):** a definite-assignment pass
    (`va_codegen::lower::persistent_vars`) marks every variable that is assigned somewhere and
    read before assignment on some path. Each gets a slot on Interface beta's existing state
    channel (`lower::PersistentVar`): every evaluation starts from its value at the last
    **accepted** timepoint (0 before any, the LRM's initial value) and proposes its final value,
    committed only for an accepted timepoint. So `load` stays pure: Newton iterations, the LTE
    controller's second solve and rejected steps see nothing they should not. DC/AC/noise never
    commit, so there such a variable starts each evaluation at 0 (and an `@(initial_step)` body
    runs in the same evaluation). A variable the setup prefix binds keeps that binding and is not
    seeded. A variable read but **never assigned anywhere** is still a build error (likely a typo).
  * **Cost, measured:** the pass is path-insensitive, so compact models get slots they never read
    (PSP103 9 of 1644 variables, BSIM4 7 of 1446, HICUM/L2 up to 56, DIODE_CMC 122 of 431), one
    `f64` per instance each, with unchanged answers: the **ten benchmark decks** (PSP103 NAND and
    inverter, c17, c432 at 8 and 1 threads, c499, c880, c17_tran) are **bit-identical to
    v2.0.0** by SHA-256; `validate` 28/28 with every error unchanged to the last digit; c432 `.op`
    11.2-12.5 s against 11.96 s (unchanged within run-to-run spread). Of the corpus, 61 of 192
    modules get at least one slot; among them the event-driven behavioural models this is for
    (`verilogaLib`'s `dff_rsn`, `pfd`, `comparator_dynamic`, `tah_ideal`, `amp_dynamic`,
    `ramp_gen`), which were silently broken until now.
  * **Stated limitations:** the lifetime is "last accepted timepoint", not "last Newton
    iteration" (the two agree for a variable that only changes at events). A persistent variable
    assigned only parameter expressions still counts as parameter-only to the charge recognizer,
    so an event switching `c` in `ddt(c * V(p, n))` does not stamp the `V * dc/dt` impulse.
    Documented in `docs/token-reference.md` (`Real`/`Integer`).
  * **Tests:** an edge counter counts 1, 2, 3, 4 exactly at the clock's falling edges and holds
    between them; an `@(initial_step)` value holds through a transient; the pass marks exactly the
    read-before-assigned variables. The SNN neuron built with the original event-based
    controller, which never fired before, fires at 1.50 and 3.75 us as the paper does.
  * **`circuits/snn/`, `models/snn/`: Xuan et al., IJCNN 2026** (`references/`, hal-05590496), a
    2T2R RRAM crossbar feeding a clocked analog leaky-integrate-and-fire neuron, built at the level
    of the paper's functional diagram (its IHP SG13S/MEMRES process is not public and it gives no
    sizes): RRAM cells at the four stated levels, C_M/C_L charge sharing (beta = 0.602 comes out of
    the circuit, it is not a parameter), clocked switches, master-slave flip-flops as switched
    capacitors, ideal op-amp/attenuator and comparator. Fig. 4 (refractory 1 us): output spikes at
    **1.50 and 3.75 us, as in the paper**, V_m within 11 mV of the paper's equations; Fig. 5
    (circuit vs snnTorch): spikes at **steps 6, 10, 14, as in the paper**, V_m within 12 mV of
    the equations and 0.14 V of the paper's transistor trace (read by eye); the 13-value synapse
    codebook within 0.0003 mS. **Open in the paper:** the attenuator K is not given and the two
    figures need different values (Fig. 4: K in [2.872, 3.047]; Fig. 5: [0.656, 0.762]), so each
    deck sets its own. `circuits/snn/README.md` has the choices and limitations;
    `docs/examples/snn_memristor/` (regenerated by `circuits/snn/make_examples.py`) the plots, the
    flattened netlists (each checked byte-identical in output to its deck) and every model source.

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 923 passed (920 + 3 new); `validate` 28/28
(convergence 28/28); corpus `check external --codegen` 200/224 (182/191 self-contained),
unchanged; zoo `check models --codegen` 41/41 (36 + the 5 SNN models); clippy and fmt clean.

2.0.0+1 - 2026-09-27
--------------------
**A benchmark to paste on each platform, and the roadmap after 2.0.** No simulator change.

  * `scripts/benchmark/`: `bench_linux.sh`, `bench_macos.sh` and `bench_windows.ps1`, each pasted
    into a terminal inside an unpacked v2.0.0 archive, with step-by-step instructions as
    comments (unpacking, clearing macOS's quarantine or Windows's download mark, a charger for a
    clean baseline). Each writes `benchmark.txt`: the machine (CPU, cores, SIMD level or P/E
    cores, memory, OS, power source and plan or governor, with battery and power-saving runs
    flagged as not a baseline), then every deck's wall time, exit code and SHA-256 of its output.
    Since 2.0 the output is platform-identical, so equal hashes across the three files confirm
    it on real machines. Ten decks, ~4 min: the rectifier, ring oscillator, a PSP103 NAND and
    inverter, c17, c432 (at default threads and at one thread), c499, c880 and c17's transient.
    c432's 1 ns transient and c7552 are optional.
  * Tested here before committing, and three bugs found and fixed:
    - `exec </dev/null` at the top of the Linux/macOS body **ended the pasted script at that
      line**, because `bash -s` reads the script from stdin. It exited 0 having run nothing. Only
      `va-cli` now gets `< /dev/null`.
    - PowerShell's `Start-Process` reported empty exit codes, and it **cost time**: c432's `.op`
      took 15.3 s under it against 9.2 s launched through `System.Diagnostics.Process` (10.7 s from
      bash), so it would have benchmarked itself. The script uses the Process API and copies
      stdout from the raw byte stream.
    - Times printed with the locale's decimal comma (Italian here). They now use the invariant
      culture.
    Result on this machine (i7-1185G7, Windows 11, Balanced plan, mains): the three bodies (the
    Linux and macOS ones under Git Bash) give **identical hashes on all ten decks**. c432 `.op`
    takes 9.0 s at 8 threads and 28.0 s at 1 thread; c17's transient takes 79.8 s.
  * `docs/roadmap.md`: "After 2.0 — open questions, not yet a road", which lists 2.0's stated
    limitations as candidates for the next road. It is not ratified; choosing a road is the user's call.

Measured at this version: no simulator change; 2.0.0's figures stand.


2.0.0 - 2026-09-27
------------------
**2.0: trusted at scale.** 1.0 meant the language front end and every analysis validated on small
circuits; 2.0 means that trust carried to ISCAS'85 scale. The three "Road to 2.0" items
(ratified 2026-09-26, the checklist at the bottom) are closed, each by its own gate:

  1. **The ISCAS'85 suite at transistor level matches its logic** (closed 1.27.0+5): all eleven
     circuits, c17 ... c7552 (up to 268 676 unknowns, 15 882 PSP103 instances), `.op` solved and
     every gate output and primary output correct: c17 on all 32 vectors, the rest on five each.
     About 18.8 min per vector for the whole suite on 8 threads.
  2. **A c432 transient matches its logic** (closed 1.28.0): five vectors switching through 50 ps
     edges, 1 196 800 settled samples correct, 46.8 min on 8 threads. It took breakpoint restarts
     at source corners, breakpoint merging, and the floor rule applied where Newton gives out.
  3. **Linux, macOS and Windows give identical output** (closed at this version, measured 1.31.0+1):
     CI runs 68 comparable decks, including c432's `.op` and c17's transient, on all three, and
     `deck-compare` finds them identical, across x86-64 CPUs with and without AVX-512 and on
     Apple arm64. It took the scalar dense LU (1.31.0), on top of 1.16.1-1.18.0's thread-count
     reproducibility (faer pinned sequential, libm for every transcendental, record/replay
     parallel evaluation).

What made the scale reachable, from 1.16 to here: flat evaluation tapes, parallel assembly, the
block-triangular DC solver (c432's linear solve 22.7 -> 1.2 s), the cap-first adaptive `gmin`
rescue (c432 `.op` 87.5 -> ~11 s), and BTF in the transient.

**Release archives** (tag `v2.0.0`, built by `.github/workflows/release.yml`) now carry
`benchmark/` as well: the ten third-party models and PSP103 cards the ISCAS'85 and NAND decks use,
with their license files. So the decks run from an unpacked archive. The README's download names
are updated to v2.0.0.

**Known limitations at 2.0, stated so the number is not read as more than it is:**
  * Every ISCAS'85 circuit from c432 up needs the `gmin` rescue (175-365 Newton iterations where
    a clean solve takes ~20). Plain Newton hits a singular matrix at its second iteration from
    zero or from the logic levels (`docs/proposals/initial-guess.md`); which rows go singular is
    not yet known. The 320-stage PSP103 chain (10 566 unknowns) still fails, undiagnosed.
  * The cross-platform identity covers the 68 decks whose models are committed. BSIM4 (its
    Verilog-A port is CC-BY-NC) and HICUM/L2 (no license statement) are not committed, pending
    a decision, and EKV is left out, so their three decks run only from a local `external/`.
    `nand/hisim2.net` fails everywhere (a NaN from the model on Newton's first iteration).
  * `.nodeset` implements only SPICE's starting-guess phase, not the held-node phase.
  * A transient's cost is still evaluation-dominated: 1 ns of c432 takes ~15 min.

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 920 passed; `validate` 28/28 (convergence
28/28); clippy and fmt clean. No code change since 1.31.0+1: the version, the checklist, the
roadmap, the README's archive names and the release workflow's archive contents.

Measured at this version: no code change; 1.31.0's figures stand.


1.31.0 - 2026-09-27
-------------------
**The dense solve gives the same bits on every platform: a scalar LU replaces faer's.** The fix
Road to 2.0 item 3 pointed at (1.30.0+4): the three decks that differed on macOS arm64 differed
only through faer's dense LU, whose kernels round differently on NEON than on x86-64.

  * `va_core::linsolve::solve_dense`, which every dense-path solve goes through (DC Newton, the
    transient integrator, AC's real-equivalent formulation), now factors with a scalar partial-
    pivoting LU: one multiply and one subtract per entry, in a fixed order, with left-to-right sums.
    Rust neither fuses `a - f*b` into an FMA nor reorders a float reduction, so vectorising the
    row update cannot change a result. It is the kernel the BTF diagonal blocks have used since
    1.21.0 (moved to `linsolve::lu_solve_in_place` and shared). A multiplier that is exactly zero
    skips its row. faer's dense LU remains only as `solve_dense_faer`, the benchmark reference.
  * Tests: the scalar kernel agrees with faer to 1e-12 on sizes 1-150, and **a golden-bits
    test** pins the exact bit patterns of a 12x12 solve recorded on x86-64 Windows. CI runs it on
    Linux and on macOS arm64.

**Speed cost, measured** (`cargo run --release -p xtask -- bench-dense`, new; per solve, i7-1185G7,
one thread, scalar / faer):

| n | ladder | mesh | dense |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 10 | 0.36 | 0.39 | 0.39 |
| 50 | 0.36 | 0.80 | 1.31 |
| 100 | 0.82 | 0.94 | 1.91 |
| 200 | 0.82 | 0.94 | 3.02 |
| 400 | 0.48 | 0.65 | 2.49 |
| 800 | 0.35 | 0.50 | 3.87 |
| 1600 | 0.25 (22 vs 87 ms) | 0.36 (33 vs 91 ms) | **9.53 (840 vs 88 ms)** |

On MNA-shaped matrices (ladder, mesh) the scalar kernel is **faster** at every size: the
zero-multiplier skip does far less work than a full dense factorisation. On a fully dense matrix
it is slower from n = 50, and 9.5x at n = 1600. The dense path is the default only below 100
unknowns (above that the sparse/BTF path runs), where a fully dense Jacobian costs at most 1.9x.
**End to end** (the CI deck set, 72 decks incl. c432 `.op` and c17's transient, min of 2 rounds
per deck): 104.8 s at 1.30 against 105.2 s at 1.31, within noise.

**Answers that moved** (deck-compare 1.30 -> 1.31: 62 identical, 9 different), as a change of
rounding moves them:
  * round-off: `hicum_output_family` (1.6e-28), `fiber_mzi_noise` (1.1e-22), `nand/lutsoi` and
    `nand/bsimbulk107` (<= 1e-16 absolute), `nand/bsimcmg` (1e-14);
  * within Newton's tolerance: `nand/bsimsoi` V(out) by 1.6e-5 V of 1.18 V; the two PSP103
    inverter transients' internal branch rows by <= 1e-11 A;
  * `ring_osc`: an oscillator's phase is sensitive to its last digits. 2 246 -> 2 243 points, so
    compared point by point it "differs" by 0.83 V; resampled on a common timebase the RMS
    difference is 2.0e-4 V and the last edge moves 0.9 us in 0.2 s. `validate` still passes it
    against QSPICE (error 4.55e-6, tolerance 1e-3).

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 920 passed (+2 linsolve tests; +1 xtask test since 1.30.0: the
numeric bound); `validate` 28/28; 1.31 against itself 71/71 identical; clippy clean.

**Road to 2.0 item 3: the macOS difference's cause, isolated.** No code change. 1.30.0+3's CI run:

  * The three decks macOS arm64 differed on, rerun with `--solver sparse`: **Linux vs macOS 3
    identical, 0 different.** The difference is the dense solve, faer's dense LU, whose kernels
    on arm64 (NEON) round differently from x86-64's. Model evaluation, the sparse/BTF solve and
    the integrator give the same bits on both.
  * The full set: Linux vs Windows 68 identical again, and this time the Windows runner had
    AVX-512 (EPYC 9V45, `avx512f` reported) while Linux did not (EPYC 7763). On x86 the dense
    kernels agree with and without AVX-512. Linux vs macOS: the same 3 bounded differences as
    1.30.0+2.

Item 3's gate, "identical, or every difference explained and bounded, in writing", is met on this
evidence. It is **not ticked here**: all three items closed makes 2.0.0 due, and releasing 2.0
is the user's decision. The option that would make macOS identical too: route the dense path
through a platform-independent scalar LU, as the BTF blocks already are. That moves every
dense-path answer in its last digits and has a speed cost to measure (docs/roadmap.md, item 3).

Measured at this version: no code change; 1.30.0+3's figures stand.

So `deck-compare` now reports, for each differing deck, the largest absolute difference over
every differing numeric token, the largest relative difference among values above 1e-15 (below
that, both sides are round-off at zero and a ratio means nothing), and the count of non-numeric

1.30.0 - 2026-09-27
-------------------
**`.nodeset`: a starting point for the DC solve, and a measurement showing that on ISCAS'85 it
does not help** (`docs/proposals/initial-guess.md`, recommendations awaiting the user's decision).

  * `.nodeset V(<net>)=<value> ...` (SPICE's card) is parsed into `Netlist::nodesets`. `va-cli`
    starts the `.op` solve, and the operating point before a transient, from those values; every
    other unknown starts at 0. With no nodeset the call is exactly the old one, so every existing
    deck is bit-identical. A nodeset naming a missing net is an error; a malformed pair is
    skipped with a note. **Limitation:** only SPICE's second phase, the starting guess; the first
    phase, which holds each node at its value, is not implemented.
  * `gen_iscas.py ... --nodeset` writes every net pin at its logic level for the deck's starting
    vector (opt-in; no nodeset deck is committed). `xtask ladder ... --current [--nodeset]`
    replays the rescue from either start, reports plain Newton's error, and with `--nodeset`
    tries warm starts: capped plain Newton from the hint, unshunted or with a shunt ramp from
    1e-6 S or 1e-9 S.

**Measured** (nine circuits, c432 ... c6288, one vector each): from the logic levels the answers
are identical to every printed digit, and so is the effort. The rescue takes the same number of
Newton iterations on 8 of 9 (c6288 227 -> 225), and wall times are equal within noise. Plain
Newton fails at its second assembly with a singular matrix whatever the start; on c432 it fails
the same way from the logic levels with a 1e-6 S or 1e-9 S shunt on every node, and with BTF
off. The starting point is not what plain Newton lacks; which rows go singular is the next
question (the doc, §5). **c7552 was not measured:** the run was stopped by Claude Code for low
system memory and was not restarted. A stray `xtask ladder` from that run's still-running shell
held ~4 GB until it was found and ended.

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 917 passed (+1 `va-netlist`, +1 `va-cli`
nodeset tests); `validate` 28/28; deck-diff against 1.29.0+1's `va-cli`: 70 identical, 0 different (13 slow decks skipped).

1.29.0 - 2026-09-27
-------------------
**Road to 2.0 item 3, the tooling: platforms compared deck by deck, on CI.** No simulator change.

  * `cargo xtask deck-run <va-cli> <out-dir> [--all] [--include <deck>]... [--skip <deck>]...`
    runs deck-diff's deck set through one binary and writes one file per deck (exit code, stdout,
    stderr without the timing `estimate:` lines) — deck-diff's comparison, split so the halves
    can run on different machines. Output is streamed to disk as it is produced: holding c432's
    transient output in memory is what a local `deck-diff --all` was killed for.
  * `cargo xtask deck-compare <dir-a> <dir-b>` compares two such trees and fails on any
    difference or any deck present on one side only. A deck that **fails on both sides** is
    listed as such and not counted as a match (1.26.1's lesson: four never-run decks compared
    "identical" all day); its stderr is not compared, since an OS error message differs by
    platform. Unit-tested on each case.
  * CI: job `repro` runs `deck-run` on Linux, Windows and macOS with its own release `va-cli`
    (deck-diff's default set plus c432 `.op` and c17's transient), uploads the tree and the
    host's `rustc -vV`/`uname -a`; job `repro-compare` compares Linux with Windows and with macOS.
    Decks whose model is not committed (BSIM4, EKV, HICUM — `benchmark/README.md`) fail on every
    side and show up as such.

Item 3 stays open until CI has run and every difference is either gone or explained in writing.
Expected risk, stated before the first run: faer dispatches SIMD kernels at run time by CPU
(AVX2, AVX-512, NEON lane widths), which can change a summation order between runners.

Measured at this version: locally, `deck-run` over the CI set takes 120 s for 72 decks (71 exit 0;
`nand/hisim2.net` is the known failure), and a tree compared with itself is 71 identical, 1 failed
on both sides; `xtask` tests 40 passed.

1.28.0 - 2026-09-27
-------------------
**Road to 2.0 item 2 closed: a c432 transient matches its logic.** `c432_seq.net` (five seeded
vectors x 2 ns, all 36 inputs switching through a 50 ps PWL edge at once, `.tran 1p 10n`):
**11 570 points in 46.8 min on 8 threads; 1 196 800 settled gate-output samples, all correct,
7/7 primary outputs among them.** What stood in the way was the integrator, not evaluation cost:

  * **Sources report their corners as breakpoints, and a breakpoint restarts the integration.**
    `va-cli`'s PULSE/PWL sources implement `ModelInstance::events` and ask for a breakpoint at
    their next corner; the step that lands on one is followed by a backward-Euler step with the
    history cleared, as at `t = 0` and as SPICE does. Before, the trapezoidal rule rang at a
    corner (source currents alternating step to step, a divided difference that does not shrink
    with `h`) and c432's sequence died of timestep underflow 0.1 ps after the 2 ns corner.
  * **Breakpoints closer than `tstep_min` merge**, with each other and with `tstop`. c17_tran's
    V1 and V2 corners at "6.1 ns" are 8e-25 s apart, and its `12n` tstop is 1.6e-24 s past V1's
    last corner; with restarts those slivers underflowed (c17_tran failed at first). A step that
    would stop less than `tstep_min` short of a breakpoint goes to it.
  * **The step after a breakpoint is judged by the rows that carry state.** Its reference was
    built from the pre-corner flows, so a source's branch current (a row with no state) showed
    a jump that no step size removes; at a breakpoint that discontinuity is declared, not
    discovered.
  * **The floor rule applies where Newton gives out.** One PSP103 instance's internal branch
    row rang at 1.06x the error budget at every step size from 1e-16 s down while every row
    with state sat at 4e-10; Newton stopped converging at 7e-18 s, above the 1e-18 s floor, so
    the rule that forgives stateless rows never applied. Now a Newton failure on a step shrunk
    only for stateless rows relaxes (latched, as at the floor) and retries the last step size.
    **No unit test reaches this path** — it needs a model whose Newton fails above the floor;
    c432_seq is its check.
  * `gen_iscas.py seq <T> <n> [<seed>] [<edge>]` writes a vector-sequence deck;
    `check_iscas.py tran <bench> <output>` checks every settled sample of every reported gate
    output (`report <bench>` prints the list). `c432_seq.net` added to deck-diff's slow list.

**Answers that moved** (deck-diff against 1.26.0, 11 of 70 decks, all transient, all exit 0 on
both sides): runs now land on source corners and restart there — point counts change
(`psp103_inverter_tran` 1898 -> 1740, `rc_pulse` 1076 -> 1021), the motorway decks land exactly on
the 900 s corner (`V(dr)` 500.000000 instead of an overshoot to 500.000376); and several decks
lose one point: the old runs ended with a duplicated `tstop` sample a sub-floor sliver apart
(`rc_discharge` printed t=5.000000e-3 twice). A correction, not a regression.

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 913 passed (+4 integrator tests, each checked
to fail without its fix, + 1 `va-cli` source-corner test); `validate` 28/28; clippy clean;
c17_tran 81 s, 16 860/16 860 settled samples correct; c432_tran (1 ns) 894 s (1.26.0: 887 s).

1.26.1 - 2026-09-26
-------------------
**Fix: `xtask deck-diff` ran four decks with a garbage `--model`, and reported them identical.**
The rule was "the word after the first `--model` in the deck file". Four decks mention `--model`
in prose — `diode_noise.net` ("no `--model` is used"), `lib_tee.net` ("`--model` takes a
directory"), `resistor_noise_table.net` and `resistor_noise_va.net` ("(via --model)") — so they
were run with a model path of `` ` `` or `),`, failed to load under both binaries alike, and every
comparison counted that as a match. They were never compared by deck-diff; `validate` (the three
noise decks, with explicit models) and a `va-cli` unit test (`lib_tee`) did exercise them.

Now the deck's own documented command line decides (`-- sim <deck> …`, continued lines joined;
the one naming the deck preferred): its `--model`, or **none** if it has none — `diode_noise.net`
must run without one, its diode resolving to the hand-written reference model on purpose. A deck
that documents no command line falls back to an existing path after `--model`, else `models`.
`lib_tee.net` and `diode_noise.net` gained usage lines. Unit-tested on each case.

Checked for anything missed: the four decks, run by the start-of-day binary (1.16.0+2) and by
1.26.0 with the right arguments, are **identical**, exit 0.

**Final full rerun at 1.26.0** (the user's request), every deck with outputs streamed to files:
73 decks, 68 exit 0; the 5 others are these four (the old rule) and `nand/hisim2.net`, the known
failure (a NaN from the model on Newton's first iteration). Slowest: c432's 1 ns transient
886.9 s, c17's 66.3 s, c432 `.op` 10.6 s. Logic checks: c17 `.op` all 32 vectors 192/192 gate
outputs; c17 transient 5 540/5 540 settled samples over 11 input vectors; c432 `.op` 196/196
signals (7/7 primary outputs). Zoo `check models --codegen` 36/36; corpus 182/191 self-contained
(200 passes). `cargo xtask tutorials`: 21/21 pages rendered (40 s; Quarto warns about the
output directory being outside the project, as before).

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 908 passed (+1 `deck_model`); `validate` 28/28; clippy clean.

**Roadmap: today's record, and a proposed road to 2.0.** No code change. `docs/roadmap.md` gains
the dated entry for 1.16.1 → 1.26.0 (c432 `.op` 87.5 s on 1 thread → 11.5 s on 8, every step
gated and its answer shift recorded; the findings worth keeping) and a **"Toward 2.0 — proposed,
not ratified"** section: the theme "trusted at scale" and six candidate blockers (the ISCAS'85
suite's `.op` checked against its logic; a c432 transient checked the same way; reproducibility
measured across Linux, macOS and Windows; a machine-readable results file; `absdelay` in
transient; Interface β changes batched at the major version), each with the gate that would
close it, for the user to ratify. `docs/future_development.md` items 1 and 6 get dated status
notes (the solve's block-triangular front end; parallel evaluation shipped).

Measured at this version: no code change; 1.26.0's figures stand.


1.26.0 - 2026-09-26
-------------------
**Transient steps use the block-triangular solve, with a large block factored by `faer` on its
own** (`docs/proposals/transient-block-solve.md`, option A). `va_core::btf` no longer refuses a
matrix with a block over `MAX_BLOCK` (64): such a block gets its own pattern and `faer` solver,
cached with the plan, and the rest is dense or substitution as before. A block holding ≥ 90% of
the unknowns (`WHOLE_MATRIX_FRACTION`) still sends the whole matrix to `faer`, so a fully coupled
circuit keeps its exact path. `TranConfig::btf` (set from `VA_BTF`, default on) switches the
integrator's solves onto it; `SparseLu` now holds its block solver boxed.

  * **Speed:** c432's 1 ns transient (`c432_tran.net`), 8 threads, mains power: **1 300.4 s →
    877.9 s (1.48×)**; the solve had been 45% of the run at 39.6 ms per solve.
  * **Answers:** `deck-diff`, 1.24.0 vs 1.26.0: with `VA_BTF=off` 72 of 72 identical; defaults
    71 of 72 — c17's transient moves (13 462 → 13 486 steps, node voltages RMS ≤ 5.5e-5 of
    scale, edges ≤ 8.4 fs). c432's transient keeps its 2 524 timepoints, node voltages within
    7.2e-7 of scale. DC decks do not move. `validate` 28/28, errors unchanged.

**Limitations:** serial; a union that grows re-runs the large block's symbolic analysis; c17-size
transients gain nothing measurable (their solve is 0.1–0.2 ms).

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 907 passed (+1); `validate` 28/28, errors unchanged;
clippy clean.


1.25.0 - 2026-09-26
-------------------
**A c432-size transient deck, and the measurements that decide the transient solve**
(`docs/proposals/transient-block-solve.md`). No simulator change.

  * `circuits/benchmark/iscas85/c432_tran.net`: c432 with its 36 inputs pulsing, `.tran 1p 1n`
    — 1 ns because a nanosecond already takes ~20 min. `gen_iscas.py … tran [<tstop>]` writes
    it (default still 12 ns). In `xtask deck-diff`'s slow list (run with `--all`).
  * `cargo xtask btf --time-tran-block-solve`: a hybrid block solve (large blocks through
    `faer` alone, the rest densely) on the transient companion matrix `G + C/h`, against `faer`
    on the whole matrix.
  * **(A):** c432's 1 ns transient, 8 threads: 1 306.9 s, of which evaluation 686.3 s (52%) and
    the linear solve **585.5 s (45%)** at 39.6 ms a solve — unlike c17 (9%). Its companion
    matrix is one 4 882-unknown block beside 10 534 singletons; the hybrid solves it in 4.1 ms
    against `faer`'s 33 ms. Decided: build it (1.26.0).
  * **(B):** c17's serial solve slows steadily with the pool size (0.115 ms at 1 thread →
    0.18 ms at 6–8), not at 8 alone; its whole cost is ~2.8 s of ~80 s and fewer threads lose
    more in evaluation. Decided: no change.

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 906 passed (+1 hybrid-solve test); `validate` 28/28; clippy clean.


1.24.0+1 - 2026-09-26
---------------------
**Proposal: what to speed up in a transient run — measured on c17**
(`docs/proposals/transient-solve.md`). No code change. c17's 0–12 ns transient (420 unknowns,
13 462 points), timers placed temporarily around the integrator's assembly and solve: at 8
threads, **evaluation 64.4 s (85%)** of 75.5 s, the linear solve 6.9 s (9%); at 1 thread 147.8 s
and 4.6 s of 156.1 s. So the transient solve is not the bottleneck at this size — a block solver
for it (its matrix keeps one 180-unknown block) could save at most ~7 s. Also found: the serial
solve is 50% slower while evaluation runs on 8 threads (0.115 → 0.172 ms, likely rayon workers
still spinning); evaluation scales only 2.3× with ~30 instances; one assembly in four is the
post-accept evaluation that commits charges and state. Options (B)–(E) listed; nothing decided.

Measured at this version: no code change; the figures are the proposal's.


1.24.0 - 2026-09-26
-------------------
**The DC rescue walks its `gmin` ramp adaptively** (`docs/proposals/adaptive-gmin.md`). Same
ends as before — 1e-3 S first, exactly 0 last — and the same starting step (1/30 of the ramp),
but a stage that converges in ≤ 8 Newton iterations doubles the next step (up to a quarter of the
ramp), and a stage that fails is retried from the last converged point with half the step; after
three halvings in a row the solve fails with that stage's error. The fixed ramp had no back-off:
one failed stage ended the ladder.

  * `NewtonConfig::gmin_adaptive` (default `false`, so a caller asking for `gmin_steps` still
    gets equal stages); `dc::with_gmin_rescue` switches it on for every ladder tier.
    `convergence::gmin_at` gives the ramp at any position. The schedule is a function of a stage
    closure, unit-tested on its own: growth, back-off and retry from the same point, the
    unshunted end, and failure at the step floor; plus the 20-diode chain agreeing with the
    fixed ramp.
  * The fast-stage threshold was measured, not guessed: 4 → c432 281 assemblies (its stages
    mostly converge in 5), 6 → 245, 8 → 228; the chains solved at every value.
  * **Answers:** `deck-diff --all` against 1.23.0, 68 of 72 identical; the four that move are
    the rescue's decks — c432 (140 of 15 416 values, the largest a 3.6e-14 A branch current in
    its last digit, no node voltage), `bsimsoi`/`lutsoi` (≤ 9.8e-15 of scale), c17's transient
    (13 487 → 13 462 steps, node voltages RMS ≤ 4.4e-5 of scale, edges ≤ 7.5 fs). `hisim2` fails
    as before. `validate` 28/28, errors unchanged.
  * **Speed:** c432 `.op`, 8 threads, mains power, two interleaved rounds: 14.96 / 14.55 s →
    **11.66 / 11.28 s (1.3×)**; 293 → 228 Newton iterations. PSP103 chains (`xtask ladder`):
    109–110 → 54–62 iterations. Over today's releases c432 has gone 80.5 s (1.18.0, 1 thread;
    87.5 s at 1.16.0) → 11.5 s on 8 threads.

**Limitations:** tuned on five decks of one technology; the back-off path is exercised by a
scripted test, not by any deck (none failed a stage); a stage failing for a reason a smaller step
cannot fix is retried three times first.

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 905 passed (+2); `validate` 28/28, errors
unchanged; clippy clean.


1.23.0+1 - 2026-09-26
---------------------
**Proposal: adaptive `gmin` stepping in the DC rescue** (`docs/proposals/adaptive-gmin.md`).
No code change in this release; the implementation is 1.24.0. The fixed rescue ramp takes 30
equal steps whatever the circuit does, and fewer equal steps fail outright on three PSP103
chains. Proposed: the same ends, with the step doubled after a stage that converges quickly and
halved — from the last converged point — after one that fails. Measured with `xtask ladder` on
the five rescue decks: 39–50% fewer Newton iterations on the chains, 22% on c432, every deck
solved, answers within the Newton tolerance. Decided in advance: the rescue's default if it won
everywhere, a switch off by default otherwise; it won everywhere.

Measured at this version: no code change; the figures are the proposal's.


1.23.0 - 2026-09-26
-------------------
**The DC rescue tries the node-step-capped `gmin` ladder before the plain one**
(`docs/proposals/dc-rescue.md`). New tier order after a failed plain solve: ladder + cap →
ladder → ladder + damping. On every circuit measured that needs the rescue the capped ladder is
never more expensive, and where the plain ladder fails it fails slowly — c432 spent 265 Newton
iterations in it before the capped ladder converged. The plain ladder stays as the next tier, so
a circuit the cap cannot solve and the ladder can is still solved (unit-tested).

  * **Answers:** `deck-diff --all` against 1.21.0, 71 of 72 identical — the four NAND sweeps
    whose failing points the plain ladder rescued reach the same printed digits, `hisim2` fails
    as before, c432 takes the same path. c17's transient moves (its starting point was rescued by
    the plain ladder): 13 450 → 13 487 steps, node voltages resampled RMS ≤ 5.9e-5 of scale,
    edges ≤ 12 fs. PSP103 chains of 40/80 stages (not under `circuits/`): ≤ 1.6e-16 V.
  * The "earlier tiers keep their bits" property the old order was built for now protects plain
    Newton alone — where almost every circuit is solved.
  * **Limitation, now wider:** the cap is 0.5 of a node's own unit; on a thermal or optical node
    that may slow a rescue that the plain ladder would have done faster. The plain ladder is
    reached only if the capped one fails, not if it is slow.

**Gate and speed:** the real build is identical on all 72 decks to the scratch build the
proposal measured (`deck-diff --all`), so its 1.21.0 comparison above carries over. c432 `.op`,
8 threads, mains power, two interleaved rounds: **28.3 / 29.3 s → 15.2 / 15.4 s (1.9×)**, from
558 Newton iterations to 293. Since 1.18.0 that deck has gone 80.5 s → 15.3 s.

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 903 passed; `validate` 28/28, errors
unchanged; clippy clean.


1.22.0 - 2026-09-26
-------------------
**Proposal: a cheaper DC rescue — run the node-step-capped ladder first**
(`docs/proposals/dc-rescue.md`), and **`cargo xtask ladder <deck> [--model <m>]`**, which replays
the rescue as `newton::solve` calls under alternative schedules, counting assemblies and
comparing answers. No simulator change.

Why c432 needs 558 Newton iterations: plain Newton goes singular in 3; the `gmin` ladder then
spends **265 iterations and fails** (one stage burns its whole 150-iteration budget in a 2-cycle,
proposing steps up to 5.8e9 V on a 1.8 V circuit); the node-step-capped ladder then converges in
290 — 47% of the iterations are a tier that fails. Measured on the five `.op` decks that need
the rescue: running the capped ladder first is never more expensive and takes c432 from 558 to
**293 assemblies (26.8 → 14.1 s)**, bit-identical where the cap tier already solved; fewer,
larger gmin steps (10/15/20 instead of 30) fail outright on three of the five decks; an early
stall cut has no clean signal in the data. A scratch build with the order swapped: `deck-diff
--all` 71 of 72 identical (NAND sweeps and `hisim2` included); c17's transient moves (its start
point is rescued by the plain ladder) by RMS ≤ 5.9e-5 of scale, edges ≤ 12 fs. Decided: cap first.

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 903 passed; `validate` 28/28; clippy clean.


1.21.0 - 2026-09-26
-------------------
**DC solves use a block-triangular (BTF) solver before `faer`'s sparse LU — Step 3 of
`docs/proposals/btf-solver.md`.** A DC logic Jacobian is almost block triangular by value, so
`va_core::btf` permutes it into its diagonal blocks (maximum transversal + Tarjan SCC), factors
each densely with partial pivoting, and substitutes in block order. The blocks come from the
**running union** of every entry that has been nonzero in a solve so far, and are recomputed only
when the union grows (measured: c432's union is fixed after the second Newton iteration, though
its nonzero set changes on almost every other one). The system goes to `faer` instead — for that
solve only, with no change in what "failed" means — when the union has no full transversal, a
block exceeds `MAX_BLOCK` (64), a block is numerically singular, or the block answer fails the
residual check every sparse answer passes.

  * `SparseLu::with_btf()` and `BtfStats`; `NewtonConfig::btf` (default on) — every DC Newton
    solve on the sparse path, i.e. above 100 unknowns: `.op`, `.dc` sweeps, and the operating
    point that starts a transient, AC or noise run. The transient integrator's own per-step solves and
    AC keep `faer` alone (their matrices keep one large block).
  * `VA_BTF=off` selects `faer` alone. `--logfull` reports `btf=` per iteration and
    `btf_solves=`/`btf_fallbacks=` per stage (`docs/validation.md`).

**Gates:** `deck-diff --all` against 1.18.0 (the last release that changed a number):
with `VA_BTF=off`, 72 of 72 identical — the `faer` path is untouched; with BTF on, 70 of 72
identical. The two that move are the only decks under `circuits/` whose DC solve is sparse:
c432 (329 of 15 416 printed values; one 4.1e-13 A branch current in its 7th digit, the rest
numerical zeros, no node voltage) and c17's transient, whose starting operating point moves in
its last digits and so its step sequence (13 538 → 13 450 points; node voltages resampled RMS
≤ 6.6e-5 of scale, edges ≤ 12 fs). `validate` 28/28, errors unchanged (no golden deck reaches
the sparse path). New unit tests: agreement with `faer`, a pivot inside a block, the same
`Singular` as `faer`, a too-large block giving `faer`'s bits exactly, the union re-analysing
only when it grows, and a 150-stage feed-forward Newton solve agreeing with BTF on and off —
with the block path shown to answer its system.

**Speed,** 8 threads, mains power, two interleaved rounds. c432 `.op`: the block path answered
557 of 558 solves (one handed to `faer`); total solve **22.7 s → 1.20 s** (`--logfull`);
wall **45.5 / 44.6 s → 28.0 / 27.9 s (1.6×)**. chain160 `.op`: 4.36 / 4.30 → 2.87 / 2.79 s
(1.5×). The proposal estimated ~22 s for c432; what is left is almost all device evaluation
(558 Newton iterations over 38 gmin stages — option (C) of the proposal is next).

**Limitations:** serial; a block over `MAX_BLOCK` sends the whole matrix to `faer`, not that
block; the answer moves in the last digits against `faer` (accepted, 2026-09-26).

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 903 passed (+6 `btf`); `validate` 28/28,
convergence 28/28; clippy clean.


1.20.0 - 2026-09-26
-------------------
**`cargo xtask btf` gains `--track-newton` and `--time-block-solve`: Steps 1–2 of
`docs/proposals/btf-solver.md`, measured (§6.1). Verdict: go.** No simulator change.

  * `--track-newton` re-runs the deck's DC solve exactly as `va-cli` does, every instance
    wrapped (all ten `ModelInstance` methods forwarded) to record each assembly's Jacobian.
    c432: the nonzero set changes on 251 of 557 iteration steps (147 distinct sets), so caching
    a BTF per set would not hold — but the **running union stops growing at the second
    assembly** and its blocks are the final ones (12 597, largest 34). chain160: same, largest 5.
    Blocks will come from the running union.
  * `--time-block-solve` is a prototype block solver: transversal + Tarjan once, then per solve
    dense LU with partial pivoting per block, substitution in block order, and the residual check
    `SparseLu::solve` makes. Same operating-point matrix, median of 5: **c432 1.24 ms vs `faer`
    29 ms (23×)**, chain160 0.40 vs 8 ms (20×); max |x − x_op| 4.0e-13 vs `faer`'s 5.7e-13.
    Estimated (not measured end to end): c432's 8-thread `.op` 43.6 → ~22 s.

**Limitations:** the prototype is not the solver — it does not handle a union entry that is zero
in one iteration making a block singular, nor the gmin diagonal; Step 3 must.

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 897 passed (+1 block-solve test); `validate`
28/28, convergence 28/28; clippy clean.


1.19.1 - 2026-09-26
-------------------
**Proposal: a block-triangular (BTF) front end for the sparse DC solve**
(`docs/proposals/btf-solver.md`). No simulator change. At 8 threads the linear solve is about
half of a large DC run (c432 22.7 of 43.6 s; chain160 1.62 of 3.55 s) and, being serial, does
not shrink with cores. By value, a DC logic matrix is almost triangular: c432 splits into
10 829 blocks, the largest 52 unknowns, whose dense factorization is 1.6 × 10⁶ flops (~1 ms)
against `faer`'s 40 ms per solve. Proposed: (A) BTF on the nonzero pattern, small blocks dense,
large ones through `faer` — measured in `xtask` before anything enters `va-core`. The BTF itself
costs 7 ms on c432, so it must be cached; how often the nonzero set changes during a Newton
solve is the first measurement. Expected (not measured): ~20 s off c432 at 8 threads.
Decided: a last-digit shift in DC answers is acceptable for this.

`cargo xtask btf` now also prints, per pattern, the unknowns in blocks larger than one, the
dense-block LU work Σ s³/3, and how long the BTF itself took. `va-core`'s `sparse.rs` header
said the sparse path was "not wired into any analysis yet" — untrue since the sparse-solve
steps landed; it now says which analyses use it and why, and points at the BTF proposal.

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 896 passed; `validate` 28/28, convergence
28/28; clippy clean.


1.19.0 - 2026-09-26
-------------------
**`cargo xtask btf <deck> [--model <m>] [--time-lu]`: how far a circuit's Jacobian splits into
block triangular form** — Step 4's first measurement (`docs/proposals/parallel-assembly.md`
§5.1.1). It assembles the deck at its DC operating point, takes a maximum transversal and the
strongly connected components of the permuted graph, and reports blocks, sizes and dependency
depth for the stored pattern and for the numerically nonzero one (DC `G`; transient `G` or `C`),
each also as a union over four bias points. `--time-lu` times `faer`'s LU with and without the
exact zeros. New public `va_cli::instances` (a deck's instances and dimension, for tooling).

What it found, c432 (15 416 unknowns): the stored pattern is one block (99.7%); the DC nonzero
pattern is **10 829 blocks, the largest 52 unknowns**, 72 deep; transient keeps one block of
43%. chain160: 4 024 blocks of ≤ 9, 324 deep; transient 39%. Explicit zeros are *not* the LU's
cost (c432 34 → 31 ms per solve without them). At 8 threads the linear solve is now about half
of each run: c432 22.7 s of 43.6 s (558 iterations × 40.7 ms), chain160 1.62 s of 3.55 s — the
next lever, and the subject of the BTF-solver decision document.

Also fixes 1.18.0's `Cargo.lock`, committed with the crates still at 1.17.0 (the version was
bumped after the last build); `cargo build --locked` would have refused it.

**Limitations:** four bias points are evidence, not proof, that an entry is zero everywhere; the
DAG depth counts blocks, not work.

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 896 passed (893 + 3 for `btf`); `validate`
28/28, convergence 28/28; clippy clean.


1.18.0 - 2026-09-26
-------------------
**Device instances are evaluated in parallel, with results bit-identical to serial evaluation
at every thread count.** Step 3 of `docs/proposals/parallel-assembly.md`. `va_core::par::load_all`
evaluates each instance into its own stamp recording on rayon's pool, then replays the
recordings into the system in instance order, so the system receives exactly the serial loop's
calls in the serial loop's order. Used by every DC assembly (dense and sparse) and every
transient assembly; AC and noise load once per analysis and stay serial.

  * **§6 interface change, ratified 2026-09-26:** `ModelInstance: Send + Sync`
    (`docs/interfaces.md`). `va-codegen`: shared model and gradient buffers `Rc` → `Arc`, lazy
    setup `RefCell<Option<_>>` → `OnceLock<_>`; one `va-transient` test double `Cell` →
    `AtomicUsize`. Per-evaluation scratch is untouched.
  * **When it goes parallel is measured, not a count:** thread hand-off costs ~40 µs per
    assembly here, which made the 13-instance reference-model `ring_osc` 2.2× *slower* in
    parallel (0.54 vs 0.24 s) while c17's ~30 PSP103 transistors ran 2.0× *faster* (82 vs
    165 s, 8 threads, two runs each). So `load_all` times a serial assembly now and then
    (first call, then every 256th), keeps an evaluation-time-per-instance estimate, and goes
    parallel when `instances × estimate` ≥ 200 µs (`PARALLEL_MIN_WORK_NS`). The choice
    depends on timing; the result does not.
  * `va-cli sim --threads <n>` sizes the pool (default: one per logical core, or
    `RAYON_NUM_THREADS`). `VA_PARALLEL=auto|always|never` forces the path, for verification.
  * `cargo xtask deck-diff` gained `--env-old K=V` / `--env-new K=V`, to compare one binary
    with itself under two environments — the gate below.

**Gate:** `deck-diff --all`, 72 of 72 decks identical in each of: 1.17.0 vs 1.18.0 (defaults);
1 thread vs `VA_PARALLEL=always` at 2, 4 and 8 threads. deck-diff compares printed output; the
bit-level claim rests on the new unit tests — a replayed recording is the same `StampSink`
calls as direct stamping, for all six methods, and `load_all` in pools of 1/2/4/8 threads
hands the sink exactly the serial call sequence (compared with `==` on the `f64`s).

**Speed,** c432 `.op` (ISCAS'85: 2 458 devices, 910 of them PSP103; 15 416 unknowns), this
laptop (i7-1185G7, 4 cores / 8 threads), mains power, two interleaved rounds, same output at
every thread count: 1 thread 80.5 / 80.4 s; 4 threads 46.1 / 48.5 s (1.7×); 8 threads
43.6 / 43.7 s (1.84×). What is left is mostly serial: the numeric LU (~16 s, proposal §5.1)
and the replay. c17's transient: 165 → 82 s at 8 threads (2.0×).

**Limitations:** replay is serial and a recording is allocated per instance per assembly; the
cost estimate is one process-wide number; the numeric LU is now the largest serial cost
(Step 4 measures what a parallel one could gain before anything is built).

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 893 passed; `validate` 28/28, convergence
28/28; clippy clean.


1.17.0 - 2026-09-26
-------------------
**Every transcendental that can reach a simulated number now comes from the pure-Rust `libm`
crate, not the platform's maths library — the same bits on every OS, toolchain and thread.**
Step 2 of `docs/proposals/parallel-assembly.md`. `exp`, `ln`, `pow`, trig, hyperbolic, `hypot`,
… go through `libm` in the AD rules and constant folding, the reference models and noise
tables, junction limiting and gmin stepping, the step-size controller, the AC sweep grid and
excitation, and the sine source. `clippy.toml`'s `disallowed-methods` now rejects `f64::exp`
and its relatives, so it stays that way; tests use `libm` too. Opted out, each with a written
reason: va-cli's plots and run-time estimate, va-harness's metrics, xtask. `sqrt`/`abs`/`powi`
are untouched (IEEE 754 fixes the first two; `powi` is multiplication).

Why: MinGW's `exp`/`pow` round differently on the main thread and on any other (x87 precision
is per-thread on Windows), which rules out parallel evaluation (Step 3); and Linux, macOS, MSVC
and GNU Windows each brought their own libm, so a deck's last digits depended on the platform.
`libm` measured ≤1 ulp everywhere, including where MinGW was 8–19 ulp out, and is faster for
`exp`/`ln`/`pow` (proposal §3.3).

**Cost — a one-time shift in answers,** `deck-diff --all` 1.16.1 vs 1.17.0: 54 of 72 decks
identical, 18 differ:

  * **14 decks keep their timebase** (DC sweeps, AC, fixed-step transients, c432's `.op`):
    largest change max|Δ| / (the quantity's own max |value|) = 1.4e-9 (bsimbulk107 NAND), and
    8.0e-7 for the rectifier's sine source; the rest ≤ 3.3e-13. Values below 1e-9 V/A (rounding
    zeros such as unused branch currents) excluded. c432: 233 of 15 416 printed values differ,
    one 4.1e-13 A branch current in its 7th digit and numerical zeros.
  * **4 adaptive transients take a different step count** — c17 13 466 → 13 538,
    `psp103_inverter_card_tran` 1 810 → 1 778, `psp103_inverter_tran` 1 861 → 1 898,
    `ring_osc` 2 243 → 2 246. Node voltages resampled onto the old timebase: RMS ≤ 3.7e-4 of
    scale (inverter `V(out)`), c17 ≤ 5.7e-5; 50%-crossing edges move ≤ 45 fs on the PSP103
    decks and ≤ 7.6 fs on c17; `ring_osc`'s edges ≤ 0.22 µs over its 0.2 s run.
  * Observed while measuring, **not changed by this release:** under the trapezoidal rule the
    c17 input sources' branch currents alternate point to point (e.g. `I(V6)` −1.44e-4 /
    −0.95e-4 A on consecutive steps after an edge) — trapezoidal ringing on a source current.
    Resampled across two different step sequences it reads as a 44% RMS "difference" that is
    not there: where both runs share a timepoint they agree to ~2e-9. Recorded for T4.

**A test was wrong, and is corrected:** `newton::gmin_stepping_converges_a_circuit_plain_newton_cannot`
claimed plain Newton fails on its 20-diode chain at *any* iteration budget (checked at 2000).
With `libm`'s `ln` in junction limiting it converges there. A sweep of 5–40 diodes × 5–160 V
found the 2000-iteration outcome flips at that one cell and nowhere else — one ulp, not a
property. What holds under either maths, over a contiguous region, is failure within the
default budget of 100 while gmin stepping succeeds; the test now asserts that. `dc.rs`'s rescue
test, which only ever relied on the default budget, is unchanged apart from its comment.

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 891 passed; `validate` 28/28, convergence
28/28, every gate's error against QSPICE identical to 1.16.1's as printed; clippy clean.


1.16.1 - 2026-09-26
-------------------
**Fix: the same deck no longer gives different last digits on machines with different core
counts.** `faer`'s solvers read a process-global parallelism setting whose default is "rayon,
all cores", and a parallel factorization orders its floating-point sums by the pool size. Every
`va-core` solve (`solve_dense`, `solve_sparse`, `SparseLu::solve`) now pins it to `Par::Seq`
first (`linsolve::pin_sequential`). Taken out of the parallel-assembly proposal's Step 2 (§3.4)
and shipped alone, because it is a bug on main, not part of that feature; `libm` and the
parallel evaluator stay in the stash.

  * chain160 (5 286 unknowns, PSP103): before, 159 of 5 292 printed lines differed between
    `RAYON_NUM_THREADS=1` and `=4`; now 1, 4 and 8 threads print byte-identical output.
  * **Answers move, within rounding noise:** 156 chain160 lines differ from 1.16.0+2 (at any
    thread count — `faer`'s 1-thread rayon path is not its sequential path). All are branch
    currents that are numerical zeros, largest change 3.2e-34 A; no value above 1e-20 moved.
    `deck-diff --all` 1.16.0+2 vs 1.16.1: 71 of 72 decks identical (the small ones go dense and
    `faer` never split them). c432 differs in 212 of 15 416 printed values: two are one
    4.1e-13 A branch current moving in its 7th (last printed) digit, the rest numerical zeros
    ≤ 6.3e-30 A.
  * Sequential is also the faster setting here (c432 numeric LU 16.0 s seq vs 36.5 s 8-way,
    proposal §5.1) — not re-timed for this release.
  * New gate `solve_is_bit_identical_whatever_the_thread_count` (va-core): a 40 000-unknown
    grid solved in rayon pools of 1, 2 and 4 threads must agree bit for bit. Without the pin,
    300 entries differ at 2 threads; a 25 600-unknown grid does not split and would not have
    caught it — so the test is big, ~15 s in a debug build. `rayon` is a va-core
    dev-dependency only (it was already in the build via `faer`).

**Limitation:** the setting is process-global, so it also makes any other `faer` user in the
same process sequential (there is none in this workspace).

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` 891 passed (890 + the new gate); `validate`
28/28, convergence 28/28; clippy clean.


1.16.0+2 - 2026-09-25
---------------------
**Proposal: evaluate device instances in parallel, reproducibly** (`docs/proposals/parallel-assembly.md`).
No code change. A change of strategy: the single-thread evaluator work of 1.13.0–1.16.0 stops here,
and the next lever is parallelism, after `references/eespice.pdf` (EEspice, graph-coloured
parallel stamping). The proposal takes EEspice's simpler variant — evaluate every instance in
parallel into its own stamp buffer, replay the buffers in instance order — because our evaluation
dwarfs our stamping and replay keeps results bit-identical, which colouring does not.

Behind it, a prototype that is **not committed** (a local branch and stash):

  * Serial vs parallel: all 72 decks identical (`deck-diff --all`, incl. c432 and c17's transient);
    chain160's 5 286 unknowns bit-identical at 1, 2, 4 and 8 threads. That needed two fixes the
    proposal carries: MinGW's `exp`/`pow` give different bits on the main thread and on any other
    (x87 precision is per-thread on Windows) — replaced by the pure-Rust `libm` crate, ≤1 ulp
    everywhere measured and faster for exp/ln/pow; and `faer`'s default parallel LU rounds
    according to the core count.
  * **That last one is a live bug in this release:** the same deck gives different last digits on
    machines with different core counts (chain160's output changes with `RAYON_NUM_THREADS`).
    Proposed fix: pin `faer` sequential — which also measured faster.
  * `libm` + sequential LU against 1.16.0+1: 59/70 decks identical; three adaptive transients take
    a different step count, waveform RMS ≤ 3.7e-4 of swing; `validate` 28/28, errors unchanged.
  * Speed, c432 `.op`: 87.5 s → 42.1 s on 8 threads (evaluation 62.4 → 17.5 s). After that the
    numeric LU is the largest cost; the proposal adds a parallel sparse LU (§5.1 — `faer`'s own is
    *slower* here, 16.0 → 36.5 s at 8 threads) and factorization reuse across Newton iterations
    (§5.2), both to be measured before built.

**Caution:** the timings are preliminary — one laptop, one run each, power unstable on the day
(it fell to battery at least twice). The c17 transient timing is void and to be redone. The 32-core
and whole-ISCAS figures are extrapolations. The correctness results are deterministic and firm.

Measured at this version: no code change; figures above are the prototype's, from 2026-09-25.


1.16.0+1 - 2026-09-24
---------------------
**Proposal: stop paying the system heap for every gradient** (`docs/proposals/gradient-storage.md`).
No code change. Two measurements behind it, both scratch builds, reverted:

  * A sampling profile of PSP103's `load()` with the dual-number operations kept out of line, so
    each shows under its own name: heap allocate/free 24.1 µs of 108.8 (22%); the model's own
    arithmetic — maths functions 1.2 µs and derivative loops 10.9 µs — about 11%; the rest moving,
    copying and wrapping dual numbers.
  * An upper bound on what allocation costs a real `load()`: a size-class caching allocator in
    front of the Windows heap made `bench-model` **12.9–19.0% faster** (medians; PSP103 −19.0%,
    BSIM4 −14.4%, BSIM-BULK107 −12.9%; nine interleaved runs each on mains power).

Options weighed: (A) a caching allocator for the three binaries — the measured win with no
evaluator change, but `unsafe` and so §5's sign-off; (B) a per-evaluation gradient arena in
`va-codegen` — removes allocation and most copying, safe, but a rewrite of the AD core;
(C) recycled buffers with a hand-written refcount; (D) sparse gradients. Proposed: decide A first,
re-profile, then B only if copying dominates. Nothing decided.


1.16.0 - 2026-09-24
-------------------
**Compiled models evaluate their expressions from flat instruction tapes instead of walking
trees — Stage 3 of the tree-walk proposal. Bit-identical; about 2% faster.** `va_codegen::tape`
compiles every root a lowered statement or contribution evaluates, once per compiled model, into
a post-order instruction list run on a value stack: constants, and parameters,
`$param_given`/`$port_connected` resolved at compile time; variable reads; unary, binary and pure
maths operators calling the very functions `eval` does (`ad::apply_unary`, `apply_binary`,
`pure_call1`, `pure_call2`, factored out of `eval` for this, unchanged); and a `Tree`
instruction handing any other subtree (probes, `?:`, user functions, analog operators) to `eval`.

Checked, not assumed: `CompiledModel::new_without_tapes` is the tree-walk reference; a unit test
covers every instruction kind at bias points that take both branches of a `?:`; and the new
`cargo xtask tape-check [<model.va>...]` loads the benchmark's seven CMC models with and without
tapes at 42 bias points each and compares every stamp bit for bit — 0 differing. A deliberately
wrong tape (a binary operator's operands popped in the wrong order) fails both.

Measured: per PSP103 `load()`, 3 384 of 3 441 visited nodes run as tape instructions, 99 `eval`
calls remain. `bench-model` against 1.15.0, nine interleaved runs each, two sets on mains power:
PSP103 median −1.9% and −0.8%, BSIM4 −2.5% and −0.6%, BSIM-BULK107 −2.5% and −5.0% — always
faster, by about 2%. Heap allocations per `load()` 431 → 432 (the value stack, sized once per
call).

**What it showed:** the proposal expected far more, because it read the profile's ~46 µs of
`eval` self time as dispatch overhead. It was mostly work inlined *into* `eval` — building each
node's dual number, copying variable values, dropping temporaries — which the tape still does,
one instruction at a time. The proposal now says so, and no longer recommends a JIT (Stage 4) on
this evidence: it would compile the same work. The next lever, if any, is the dual-number
representation (option D).

Also: `CodegenError::Internal`, for a broken invariant of the crate's own (a tape running out of
operands) — deliberately not `Unsupported`, which `va-cli` reports as a refusal of the user's
model. `bench-model`'s model selection is shared with `tape-check` (`model_paths`).

Measured at 1.16.0: `cargo test --workspace` **890 passed / 0 failed** (+1: the tape matches
the tree walk bit for bit). fmt clean; clippy clean with and without `walk-stats` (with it, the
tape's counts equal the tree walk's: PSP103 3 441 visited, 11.0% hoistable). `cargo xtask
validate`: **28/28**, identical to 1.12.0. `cargo xtask deck-diff` against 1.15.0: **70
identical**, 2 skipped. `va-cli check external --codegen`: output identical to 1.15.0.


1.15.0 - 2026-09-24
-------------------
**Stage 1 of the tree-walk proposal: an invariance analysis that counts what hoisting could
save, and `cargo xtask deck-diff`.** No evaluation changes: every result is bit-identical.

`va_codegen::lower::invariance` finds every expression and variable whose value is the same on
every evaluation of an instance — the dependence analysis the setup prefix does without. Variables
by a greatest fixpoint (an assignment counts only if its right-hand side *and* every condition it
sits under are invariant; anything a user-defined function writes, or is passed, is struck out
up front); expressions by the prefix's own exhaustive whitelist. `Lowered::invariance` stores the
result; nothing evaluates differently yet. Behind a new `walk-stats` feature (off by default),
`ad::eval` counts visited nodes and those strictly inside an invariant subtree —
`cargo run --release -p xtask --features walk-stats -- bench-model` adds `visited` and
`hoistable` columns.

What it found, the benchmark's seven CMC models: a hoist could skip **11.0% of PSP103's visited
nodes** (3 441 per `load()`), 11.1% BSIM-SOI, 12.5% BSIM4, 14.3% EKV2.6, 16.5% JUNCAP200, 21.5%
HICUM/L2v3, 21.7% BSIM-BULK107 — far below the 47–69% of nodes with no gradient, most of which are
leaves feeding bias-dependent operations. At the walk's ~48% of a PSP103 evaluation that is at
most ~5%. The proposal (`docs/proposals/evaluator-tree-walk.md`) now records this and recommends
the flat tape (Stage 3) before hoisting; not decided.

Why the counter is opt-in: a check on every `eval` call, even disabled, measured +6.6%, +7.0% and
+0.5% on PSP103's median in three interleaved sets. This laptop cannot settle a few percent, and
the evaluator's hottest path is not the place for an unproven cost. The analysis runs once per
model build and stays on.

`cargo xtask deck-diff <old va-cli> <new va-cli> [--all] [--skip <deck>]` runs every deck under
`circuits/` through two binaries and fails on any difference in exit code, stdout or stderr
(timing lines aside) — the check 1.14.0–1.14.0+3 ran from a scratch script. The two decks that
take minutes are skipped unless `--all`. `xtask`'s help text also lost the stray blank lines its
`bench-model` entry had.

Measured at 1.15.0: `cargo test --workspace` **889 passed / 0 failed** (+2: the analysis finds
invariant work past the first probe and leaves a subtree's root in place; and it answers
*dependent* for every way a value can move — a probe-dependent condition, a later dependent
assignment found on a later fixpoint round, a user-function argument, result, parameter and
return variable. Each of the three guards was removed in turn and the test failed each time).
fmt clean; clippy clean with and without `walk-stats`. `cargo xtask validate`: **28/28**,
identical to 1.12.0. `cargo xtask deck-diff` against 1.14.0+3: **70 identical**, 2 skipped.
`va-cli check external --codegen`: output identical to 1.14.0+3 (200 passes).


1.14.0+4 - 2026-09-24
---------------------
**Proposal: cut the evaluator's tree-walk overhead** (`docs/proposals/evaluator-tree-walk.md`).
No code change. After 1.14.0+3 the walk itself — `eval`'s recursion, dispatch, arena lookups and
per-node `Result` — is the largest cost of a PSP103 `load()`: ~46 µs of ~96 µs, ~13 ns per node
over 3 441 nodes visited per call. Measured for the proposal (scratch instrumentation, reverted):
nodes visited per `load()` — PSP103 3 441, BSIM4 3 390, BSIM-BULK 7 106 — of which 47%, 59%, 69%
carry no gradient, an upper bound on what is still recomputed every call despite not depending on
the unknowns (the setup split only takes a leading run of statements).

Proposed, in stages, each measured and bit-identical: (1) a dependence analysis that only
*counts* what could be hoisted, with a go/no-go threshold; (2) hoisting bias-independent
subexpressions beyond the prefix into setup, defended by a differential test against the plain
evaluator; (3) a flat instruction tape in place of recursive evaluation; (4) a Cranelift JIT only
if the walk still dominates, with its own proposal for §5's `unsafe` sign-off. `va-codegen` only;
Interfaces α and β untouched; the T2 owner to agree before stage 2.

Also recorded there: a pooled variable table (reuse one table per model instead of allocating
~81 KB per PSP103 call) was bit-identical and saved nothing measurable (92.1 → 91.2 µs median),
and was reverted — the table's allocation is not where local-variable time goes.


1.14.0+3 - 2026-09-24
---------------------
**A probe read's gradient is collected straight into its `Rc<[f64]>`: one allocation instead of
a `Vec` plus a copy.** The three probe reads (`V(p, n)`, a flow `I(...)`, an `idt` value) filled a
`vec![0.0; n]` and then called `Rc::from(vec)`, which allocates the `Rc` and copies — two
allocations each. They now share `probe_grad`, which maps a range straight into the `Rc`; each
slot's arithmetic is the old code's, so results are bit-identical. The arithmetic paths
(`map_owned`, `zip_owned`) were already single allocations: they collect from length-reporting
iterators, which the standard library builds into the `Rc` directly — confirmed by the counts,
988 allocations per `load()` at 1.14.0+1 against ~972 gradient vectors. `Dual::variable` keeps
its `Vec`: it is not on the solve path, and its out-of-range slot panics today, which a rewrite
would silently change.

Heap allocations per PSP103 `load()` (counting allocator): 443 → **431**, the 12 probe reads per
evaluation. Time: no measurable change — `bench-model`, seven interleaved runs, medians 90.0 µs
before and 91.1 µs after, inside the noise; 12 of 431 allocations is under 3%.

Measured at 1.14.0+3: `cargo test --workspace` **887 passed / 0 failed**. fmt + clippy clean.
`cargo xtask validate`: **28/28**, identical to 1.12.0. All 70 decks under `circuits/` give
output identical to 1.14.0+2's.


1.14.0+2 - 2026-09-24
---------------------
**Dual-number operations write their result into an operand's gradient buffer when nothing else
holds it: PSP103 `load()` a further 23% faster, 42% faster than 1.14.0.** `zip_with` is now one
implementation over owned gradients with three cases — both zero (no allocation), exactly one zero
(the rule applied to the other operand's partials alone, a single pass, in place when that buffer
is unshared), both dense (written into whichever operand's buffer is unshared). `Grad::map` (the
chain rule, `scale`, `neg`) does the same. `add/sub/mul/div/powf/neg/exp/ln/sqrt/abs` gained
by-value forms (`*_owned`) that consume their operands; the `&self` forms delegate to them with
shared handles, so a borrowed operand is never written and every other caller behaves as before.
`eval`'s arithmetic arms and `eval_call`'s `exp/ln/sqrt/abs/pow` pass their own temporaries by
value. Whether a buffer is unshared is decided by `Rc::get_mut` at run time, so a local
variable's value (shared with the variable table) is never overwritten.

Each rule keeps its own per-element function, applied to the same elements in the same order —
not reduced to two coefficients `ca·p + cb·q`, which rounds differently for `div` — so every
result is **bit-identical** and the change could be checked against every deck exactly.

Measured, all against the same session: `xtask bench-model` on PSP103's `load()`, seven runs of
each binary interleaved — 1.14.0 median 175.6 µs, 1.14.0+1 131.2 µs, **1.14.0+2 101.2 µs** (best
171.9 / 118.4 / 94.0). Heap allocations per `load()` (counting allocator, scratch build): 1 845 →
988 → **443**. In a circuit, the 160-stage PSP103 chain `.op`, five runs alternated against 1.14.0:
assembly per Newton iteration 65.2 → 40.1 ms, wall 14.0 → 10.1 s; gradient vectors allocated
88.6M → 19.9M, 26.7M results written in place (`grad_in_place`, a new counter).

Measured at 1.14.0+2: `cargo test --workspace` **887 passed / 0 failed** (the finite-difference
Jacobian checks included). fmt + clippy clean. `cargo xtask validate`: **28/28**, identical to
1.12.0. All 70 decks under `circuits/` give output identical to 1.14.0+1's.


1.14.0+1 - 2026-09-24
---------------------
**Dual-number gradients live in one shared allocation (`Rc<[f64]>`): copying a value no longer
copies its gradient, and a new gradient is one heap allocation. PSP103 `load()` is 24% faster.**
Reading a local variable copies its value; 1.14.0's counters showed those copies were 47% of every
gradient vector a PSP103 evaluation builds. A gradient is never modified after it is built, so
sharing it changes no arithmetic: every result is bit-identical.

Measured, `xtask bench-model` on PSP103's `load()`, seven runs of each binary alternated: median
172.7 → 130.2 µs, best 157.4 → 120.0 µs; the two sets do not overlap. Heap allocations per `load()`
(a counting allocator, scratch build): **1 845 → 988**.

**How it was found, because the first attempt measured nothing.** It was first written as
`Rc<Vec<f64>>` and showed no speed-up (216 → 221 µs), which I read as "allocation is not the cost"
and wrote into the docs. A sampling profile (samply, attached to `bench-model`) said otherwise:
~29% of the run inside the heap allocator, called from `Dual::mul/add/sub`, plus freeing
(`Rc::drop_slow` 10.5%). The cause was the first version itself: `Rc<Vec<f64>>` takes **two**
allocations per new gradient (the `Rc` box and the buffer), so removing 874 copies added ~970
allocations — 1 845 → 1 946 per `load()`, a net increase. `grad_allocs` counts gradient vectors,
not heap allocations, and could not see it. `Rc<[f64]>`, collected straight from the iterator, is
one. The profile and its analysis script are kept locally (`/perf-logs/`).

Still open, from the same profile: the tree walk itself (`eval` ~19% self time, `get_var`/
`set_var` ~6%) and the remaining ~990 allocations per `load()` — about 22% of which are
one-zero-operand results in `zip_with`, a full-length scaled copy that operators consuming their
operands could write in place.

Measured at 1.14.0+1: `cargo test --workspace` **887 passed / 0 failed** (the finite-difference
Jacobian checks included). fmt + clippy clean. `cargo xtask validate`: **28/28**, identical to
1.12.0. All 70 decks under `circuits/` give output identical to 1.14.0's.


1.14.0 - 2026-09-24
-------------------
**`va-cli sim` builds the circuit once instead of three times, and `--logfull` counts hash
lookups, per-call map builds and probe allocations.**

Build once: a run used to call `build_instances` separately for the pre-flight estimate, for the
solve and for the report's row labels — compiling every distinct model card again each time,
because the compiled-model cache lives inside one build. `run_sim` now builds once and shares
it. The solvers take an optional prebuilt circuit (`solve_*_impl`); the public `solve_*_with`
functions keep their signatures and build their own, so `va-harness` is unchanged. A circuit
that fails to build still reports that error where the solve used to, after the `--plot` check.
Saved ~0.8 s on c432 (0.37 s before the first Newton stage and 0.41 s after the last), ~0.12 s
on a 320-instance chain: about 0.6–1% of a long `.op`, more of a short run.

Counters (`va_codegen::counters`, `va_core::counters`): `stamp_lookups` (sparse stamping's
`(row, col) → slot` hash lookups), `ctx_maps_built` (three per compiled-instance evaluation),
`probe_allocs` (a gradient vector per `V`/`I`/`idt` read), `ctx_map_lookups`, `grad_allocs`
(every dense gradient vector the dual-number arithmetic allocates, copies included) and
`grad_clones` (the copies; `Grad`'s `Clone` is now written by hand to count them). Each `[logfull]`
line reports their increase over its iteration or stage, and a closing `[logfull] run` line the
process totals, transient timesteps included. `va-core` cannot see `va-codegen`, so the Newton
log reads the codegen counters through `NewtonConfig::log_counters`, a function pointer
`va-cli` supplies. Off by default at one relaxed atomic load per site; on, a relaxed add.

What they showed (PSP103 chains of 10–320 instances and c432, the same rates in every circuit):
every PSP103 evaluation does **~1 850 gradient allocations, ~880 of them copies** of a local
variable's value — 941 million over one c432 `.op`. Against that, 12 probe allocations, 18 map
lookups, 3 map builds, and ~79 stamp lookups per instance per iteration. These are how often
things happen, not what they cost; 1.14.0+1 acted on the biggest of them. The run logs are kept locally (`/perf-logs/`, now in `.gitignore`), not in
the repository.

Measured at 1.14.0: `cargo test --workspace` **887 passed / 0 failed** (+1:
`counters_count_evaluations_only_while_enabled`, including the gradient counters). fmt + clippy clean. `cargo xtask validate`:
**28/28**, identical to 1.12.0. All 70 decks under `circuits/` (c432 and c17's transient aside,
compared through their logs) give output identical to 1.13.0's, exit codes included.


1.13.0 - 2026-09-24
-------------------
**`va-cli sim --logfull`: a per-iteration trace of the DC Newton solve.** Every iteration prints
a `[logfull] iter` line on stderr — its tier of convergence aids (`plain`, `ladder`, `ladder+cap`,
`ladder+damp`), `gmin`, assembly time, linear-solve time, line-search trial time, nonzero count
and whether the symbolic factorization was redone (sparse path), step fraction, residual and
largest applied step — and every solve stage a `[logfull] stage` line with the iterations it took,
its outcome and its totals. `va_core::newton::NewtonConfig` gains `log_full` (default off) and
`va_cli::set_log_full`; format in `docs/validation.md` § "Reading a `--logfull` trace".

Why: splitting assembly from solve took a scratch build each time (2026-09-23), and a combined
timer gave a misleading per-instance figure; the user asked for the split to stay available for
debugging. Process-wide rather than a new argument on every `*_with` solver, because it changes
no number. **Limitation:** DC Newton only (every analysis's operating point, `.dc` points), not
the transient integrator's per-timestep loop.

What it measured (release, one run each, `docs/validation.md`): PSP103 evaluation costs
**176–204 µs per instance per iteration, flat from 80 to 910 instances**. The 0.7–0.8 ms quoted
for the chains yesterday came from the combined timer, so it was wrong. Assembly is 72–96% of
run time, the sparse solve the rest. c432 `.op` took 151 s and 557 iterations, and the plain
ladder's failed 265 iterations (75 s) were half of it. Unexplained: the sparse solve jumps 13×
between 1 326 and 2 646 unknowns on the chain.

Measured at 1.13.0: `cargo test --workspace` **886 passed / 0 failed** (+1:
`log_full_changes_no_number_on_either_path`, which requires bit-identical answers with the trace
on and off on both paths with all aids on, and the same error on a failing solve). fmt + clippy
clean. `cargo xtask validate`: **28/28**, identical to 1.12.0.


1.12.0 - 2026-09-23
-------------------
**ISCAS'85 c432 solves at transistor level, through a new DC rescue tier that caps each node's
Newton step.** `NewtonConfig` gains `max_node_step` (default infinite: off, the default path's
arithmetic unchanged). The DC rescue in `va_core::dc` is now plain → `gmin` ladder → **ladder with
node steps capped at 0.5 V** (`RESCUE_NODE_STEP`) → ladder with the line search (1.10.1's tier,
now last). Each tier runs only after the ones before it fail, so anything an earlier tier solves
keeps its path and answer bit for bit.

Why: c432 (910 PSP103 devices, 15 416 unknowns) failed every existing tier. Traced, the ladder
cycled and the damped ladder stalled on the internal nodes of 4-high NMOS stacks (the `and4` cell
inside a composed 4-input NAND), set only by leakage while a lower device is off: each step
proposed 11–23 V on a 1.8 V circuit. Capping each node separately lets every other row take its
full step. It also beats damping on the PSP103 inverter chains 1.10.1 was about: 95 stages
23.2 s → 9.6 s, 160 stages 40.8 s → 15.7 s (320 still fails, as before). The cap is in each
unknown's own units: 0.5 V electrically, 0.5 of anything else — reached only after the plain
ladder fails.

c432 (`circuits/benchmark/iscas85/`): `c432.bench` fetched and checked gate for gate against an
independent Verilog copy (160 gates, 36 inputs, 7 outputs, identical). `gen_iscas.py` turns any
`.bench` into a deck in the c7552 style; gates the library lacks are composed from its cells —
the user's rule: use the cells we have, compose the rest (a buffer is two inverters, now
supported; NAND3/4 = AND3/4 + NOT, XOR = XNOR + NOT, AND8/AND9 from AND4/AND3). `check_iscas.py`
checks an `.op` against the logic. Five vectors solve; four checked on all 160 gate outputs,
**640/640 correct**.

Wall time, release, one run each: c432 `.op` 2.9–6.9 min (the same deck and binary 6.9 min once
and 2.9 min later — a single figure is good to ~2× on this laptop); before this change it failed
after 7.1 min. Profiled: per Newton step assembly 218 ms (~70%, the PSP103 evaluation), sparse LU
88 ms (~28%). The pre-flight estimate (44–719 ms) was off by ~250–600×, as it is for any rescued
compiled-model `.op`. A c432 transient would be an estimated 3–4 h; not run.

Measured at 1.12.0: `cargo test --workspace` **885 passed / 0 failed** (+1: the node-step cap
converges a diode clamp plain Newton cannot and leaves a converging answer unchanged; the tier
test now also checks the capped tier's order). fmt + clippy clean. `cargo xtask validate`:
**28/28**, identical to 1.11.0. All 69 other decks under `circuits/` give output identical to
1.11.0's.


1.11.0 - 2026-09-23
-------------------
**The netlist parser reads SPICE deck structure — `.subckt`, `.model` cards, `+` continuations,
`.include`, `.global`, and ngspice's `N` element — and ISCAS'85 c17 runs at transistor level.**

`va_netlist` resolves the structure in a new pass before its line parser (`spice.rs`), so a deck
that uses none of it parses exactly as before:

  * `.subckt`/`.ends` are flattened, with ngspice's naming: internal net `sig3` of instance `X5`
    becomes `X5.sig3`, element `nm01` becomes `n.X5.nm01`; ports map to the instance's nets;
    `0`, `gnd` and `.global` nets stay global; definitions may follow their use and nest by
    instantiation; self-instantiation is an error.
  * `.model <name> <module> …` cards apply to `X` and `N` lines: the card names the Verilog-A
    module, its parameters come first and the line's own replace them by name; `level=` is
    dropped (SPICE's dispatch, not a parameter).
  * `N` lines (ngspice's element for an OSDI-compiled Verilog-A device) are read as `X` lines.
  * `.include` resolves relative to the including file (`va_netlist::parser::parse_file`,
    which `va-cli` now uses); from a string deck it is an error saying so.
  * An ngspice `.control` block and `.options` are skipped with a note each, which `va-cli`
    prints before solving (`Netlist::notes`).

Names from SPICE syntax — an `N` line, or anything resolved through a card — match the module's
declarations **ignoring case** (`Device::spice_names`): a card's `psp103va` and `l=` reach
`PSP103VA` and `L`. A name matching two declarations ignoring case, and neither exactly, is
refused. Plain `X` lines keep Verilog-A's exact matching.

Limitations: no subcircuit parameters (an error, not defaults), no `.subckt` defined inside
another, no `.lib`; `.model` cards do not apply to `M`/`D`/`Q` lines.

**c17** (`circuits/benchmark/iscas85/`): six NAND2 gates in the c7552 deck's style — its `nand2`
subcircuit verbatim, its PSP103 cards, RC nets with its element values (a star per net, not its
generator's trees). 24 PSP103 devices, 420 unknowns, sparse. `.op` over all 32 input vectors:
192/192 gate outputs correct (highs ≥ 1.799992 V, lows ≤ 25.3 µV). `.tran 1p 12n`: 13 466 points
in 4.1 min, all 5 528 settled output samples correct over 11 input vectors; the pre-flight
estimate (0.08–2.4 min) undershot by ~1.7×. No golden — the check is the logic function
(`check_c17.py`), since the project's oracle has no PSP103 set up. c7552 now parses in
principle but is ~250 000 unknowns; the next step is c432.

Measured at 1.11.0: `cargo test --workspace` **884 passed / 0 failed** (+8: flattening and
scoping, cards and continuations on `N` lines, `.control`/`.options` notes, `.global`,
`.include` relative to the including file, six malformed-structure errors, and end to end in
`va-cli` a subcircuit divider through a card with mis-cased module and parameter names, plus the
ambiguity refusal). fmt + clippy clean. `cargo xtask validate`: **28/28**, identical to 1.10.1.
