All 69 decks under `circuits/` give output identical to 1.10.1's (solver/estimate lines aside).


1.10.1 - 2026-09-23
-------------------
**A damped second tier for the `gmin` rescue: long device chains that went singular now solve.**
When the plain `.op` fails and the `gmin` ladder fails too, `va_core::dc` now runs the ladder
once more with Newton's residual line search on (`RESCUE_DAMPING_HALVINGS = 20`). If that fails
as well, the original error is reported, as before.

The failure 1.9.0 found: a chain of PSP103 inverters went "singular matrix" from ~95 stages
(3 141 unknowns), on the dense and sparse paths alike. 1.9.0 recorded a guess that the solve's
residual tolerance, absolute in `b`, was rejecting good solves of a large matrix. **Instrumenting it
disproved that**: every rejected solve had a componentwise backward error of 1.0 and a solution of
1e14–1e20, and iterative refinement did not help — the matrices really were numerically singular.
The cause was an undamped Newton step inside the `gmin` rescue: part-way down the ladder one step
proposed ~5.7e4 V at a net near the chain's end, the residual jumped from 1.5e-4 to 54, and the
step back landed on a singular Jacobian. Nothing limited it, because junction limiting covers only
unknowns a model marks as junctions.

Why a tier and not damping for the whole rescue: damping costs at least one extra assembly per
iteration, and switched on for the whole rescue it took the 80-stage chain (which the ladder
already solved) from 6.8 s to 35 s. As a tier, everything the ladder solves keeps its path and
its answer bit for bit.

Measured (release, `.op`, one run each): 80 stages unchanged (7.3 s, same answer); 95, 100 and
160 stages (up to 5 286 unknowns) now solve in 20–41 s, with the physical answer (input at 0 V:
odd stages end at 1.199991 V, even at 4.43 µV). **Still failing: 320 stages (10 566
unknowns)**, by a different mechanism — the Jacobian goes singular right after a 0.53 V step, not
a runaway one, which no line search helps. Not diagnosed. All 12 NAND benchmark decks give output
identical to 1.10.0 (HiSIM2 still non-finite, as before).

`cargo test --workspace` **876 passed / 0 failed** (+1: the rescue tries plain, ladder, damped
ladder in that order, a ladder success never reaches the damped tier, and a total failure reports
the first error). The physical case needs PSP103, which is not in the repository, so it is
measured and recorded (`docs/validation.md`) rather than a test. fmt + clippy clean.
`cargo xtask validate`: **28/28**, identical line for line to 1.10.0.


1.10.0 - 2026-09-23
-------------------
**The sparse threshold moves from 500 to 100 unknowns.** `va_core::sparse::SPARSE_THRESHOLD` is
100: under `--solver auto` (the default) every analysis of a circuit with 100 or more unknowns now
runs on sparse LU. Decided by the user on 1.9.0's measurement: whole analyses cross over between
~20 and ~50 unknowns on an RC ladder and an RC mesh, and at ~100 sparse is 1.9–5× faster on the
mesh and 4–48× on the ladder, so 100 sits clear of the crossover and of its run-to-run noise. On
a real device circuit the gain starts here too: a 10-inverter PSP103 chain (336 unknowns) ran
1.9× faster sparse, and was dense under the old threshold.

The cost: a circuit between 100 and 499 unknowns now gets sparse's last-digit differences from
dense (a different pivot order; every gate tolerance is far above them), and its pre-flight
estimate is quoted from the sparse tables. Nothing in the repository sits in that range — every
validation gate is at most 8 unknowns and the PSP103 benchmark decks are ~35–70 by the ~16 rows
per instance — so no committed result moves. `--solver dense` restores the old path.

Five estimate tests had pinned the 500 value by using 300 unknowns as "dense under `Auto`"; they
now force the dense path to test the dense tables, and the solver-line test uses 99/100.

Measured at 1.10.0: `cargo test --workspace` **875 passed / 0 failed** (no new tests; the
threshold test now asserts 100). fmt + clippy clean. `cargo xtask validate`: **28/28**,
identical line for line to 1.9.0.


1.9.0 - 2026-09-23
------------------
**Sparse solve, Step 5: the crossover measured, and the pre-flight estimate calibrated on the
path the run takes.** `cargo xtask bench-scale` gains `--solver auto|dense|sparse` and `--topology
ladder|mesh` — a square RC mesh beside the RC ladder, because the ladder has no LU fill-in and is
sparse's best case — and runs sparse to 6 400 nodes (dense stops at 800 by default). `va-cli`'s
estimate now quotes a sparse run from sparse tables, its bracket running from the ladder's cost
(low end) to the mesh's (high end), exponents 1 and 2 beyond 6 402 unknowns; the sparse cost line
prints `sparse matrix` instead of a dense `dim²` memory figure, and the solver line no longer
says the estimate overstates. `docs/validation.md`'s size-limit section is rewritten from the
new measurements; the 2026-09-17 dense record is kept below it. The dense tables are unchanged.

What was found, all measured today on the i7-1185G7 (release, two runs each, slower kept):

  * **The dense/sparse crossover is ~20–50 unknowns, not 500.** Dense wins only at 11–22 unknowns
    (up to 5×, by tens of microseconds per point); from ~50 sparse wins every column of both
    circuits — 1.9–12× on the mesh, 3–195× on the ladder, up to ~800 unknowns. **The threshold
    is not changed here**: that is a behaviour change for every circuit between the new value
    and 500, the user's decision, and its own release. No gate would move (the largest is 8
    unknowns).
  * **Sparse scaling:** a transient point at 6 402 unknowns costs 4.2 ms (ladder) to 36.8 ms
    (mesh); dense took 31–38 ms at ~800. Exponent ~1 on the ladder, 1.0–1.6 on the mesh.
  * **A real device circuit** (a PSP103 inverter chain, `.op`; each instance adds ~16 internal
    rows, so 15 inverters already cross 500): at 2 646 unknowns 247 MB / 63 s dense against
    30 MB / 12 s sparse; at 336 unknowns, below the threshold, sparse is already 1.9× faster.
    One run each.
  * **Not a sparse regression:** from 100 inverters (3 306 unknowns) the chain's `.op` fails as
    singular **on both paths** (dense confirmed at 160 inverters). Instrumented once and reverted:
    the `gmin` rescue's solve is rejected by the residual check, 1.2e-4 against 1e-6·(1 + |b|), a
    tolerance that does not scale with the matrix entries. Not fixed and not verified — the next
    thing to look at, since it now caps device-circuit size where the solver no longer does.
  * **The estimate's blind spot, both paths:** on that chain it undershoots by 30–1 000× (40
    inverters: 174–303 ms quoted dense vs 9.6 s; 2.2–37 ms sparse vs 2.2 s), because it is per
    Newton loop on a linear circuit and a `gmin` rescue is many loops. Documented; not knowable
    from `dim`.
  * Today's dense ladder is 1.1–2.1× faster than the 2026-09-17 tables from 102 unknowns up (not
    investigated), so the unchanged dense estimate now errs slow.

Measured at 1.9.0: `cargo test --workspace` **875 passed / 0 failed** (+3: the sparse bracket is
ordered at every size and spans ladder to mesh; the estimate follows the solver, and above the
threshold `Auto` is the sparse quote; the sparse bracket contains both calibration circuits'
faster runs). fmt + clippy clean. `cargo xtask validate`: **28/28**, identical line for line to
1.8.0 (no solve path changed).

Still open from the proposal: the threshold decision, and the one large (~5 000-unknown) QSPICE
golden §5 called for, which has not been generated.


1.8.0 - 2026-09-23
------------------
**Sparse solve, Step 4: AC and noise use it from 500 unknowns — every analysis now does.**
`va_acnoise::ac::run_with` and `noise::run_with` take a `Solver` (the old names are `Auto`), and
`va-cli`'s `solve_ac_with`/`solve_noise_with` pass `--solver` through to the frequency loop, not
only to the operating point. On the sparse path the small-signal system is the same real
embedding `[[G, -wC], [wC, G]]` the dense path solves, factored sparse; its pattern is built
once from the `G`/`C` pattern, so one symbolic factorization serves the whole sweep. The noise
adjoint embeds the plain transpose on both paths. Below 500 unknowns nothing changed.

Why the embedding and not a complex sparse LU: it solves the identical system, so dense and
sparse agree structurally, and it needed no complex counterpart of `SparseLu`. A complex LU
would be a constant factor faster (half the dimension; one factorization could also serve the
noise adjoint by a transpose solve) but brings a trap — `faer`'s transpose solve takes a `Conj`
flag, and the conjugate transpose is silently wrong above DC. Recorded as an option, not done:
`docs/future_development.md` §1a.

The pre-flight line no longer carries a per-analysis scope ("the frequency sweep is still
dense"): it now says sparse covers the whole run, for every analysis. `bench-scale`'s banner no
longer claims every row is dense LU.

Measured at 1.8.0: `cargo test --workspace` **872 passed / 0 failed** (+5: dense and sparse AC
agree on an RC low-pass, a non-symmetric G with off-diagonal C, and a frequency-dependent
circuit; `Auto` at 501 unknowns is bit-for-bit forced sparse in AC; noise spectrum, input-referred
PSD and per-device split agree on a non-symmetric circuit up to 100 MHz). fmt + clippy clean.
`cargo xtask validate`: **28/28**, output identical line for line to 1.7.0 (stash diff).
`cargo xtask validate --solver sparse`: **28/28**; one printed figure differs, in the last
digits: `rc_ac_oct` |mag| error 1.78e-15 against 1.94e-15. `bench-scale`, two runs each on
1.8.0 and on 1.7.0 in the same session: at 802 unknowns `.ac` **0.43–0.49 ms per point**
against 156–172 ms dense, `.noise` **0.38–0.46 ms** against 194–198 ms — about 350x and 450x.
Rows below 500 unknowns match 1.7.0 within run-to-run noise.

Not yet: the pre-flight cost estimate is still calibrated on dense LU and overstates sparse
runs (Step 5). And on this ladder sparse at 802 unknowns is already faster than dense at 402,
which says the crossover lies well below 500 — for a ladder, sparse's best case. Whether the
threshold moves is Step 5's measurement on denser circuits.


1.7.0 - 2026-09-23
------------------
**Sparse solve, Step 3: the transient timestep loop uses it from 500 unknowns.** `TranConfig`
gains `solver` (`Auto` by default). The integrator holds one dense-or-sparse `Linear` for the
whole run: on the sparse path the pattern is found at the seed evaluation at `tstart` and the
symbolic factorization is reused by every Newton iteration of every timestep, and the
companion matrix `J + coeff·dQ` is one pass over the values of the shared pattern.
`va_core::sparse::SparseSystem` now records `bound_step` the way `DenseStamp` does. The
`@(final_step)` probe compares the two evaluations' value arrays; if the pattern grew between
them the arrays differ in length, which counts as a change and is honoured with a re-solve (the
conservative reading). Below 500 unknowns the dense branch is unchanged, apart from one loop
rewritten as a zip that clippy asked for, with the same arithmetic. `va-cli`'s pre-flight line
now says sparse covers the whole run for `.tran`; `.ac`/`.noise` loops stay dense until Step 4.

Found while writing the tests: in a debug build, dense LU at 501 unknowns costs 0.2 s per solve,
and a 501-unknown RC run made ~500 of them. 103 s dense, 1.6 s sparse. The test comparing `Auto`
with dense at that size took three minutes; it now checks that `Auto` is bit-for-bit the
forced-sparse run, and agreement with dense is shown by the smaller tests.

Measured at 1.7.0: `cargo test --workspace` **867 passed / 0 failed** (+5: RC charge and ring
oscillator, same steps and values on both paths; `bound_step` on the sparse path; `Auto` at 501
unknowns is the sparse path; `SparseSystem`'s `bound_step`). fmt + clippy clean.
`cargo xtask validate`: **28/28**, output identical line for line to 1.4.1.
`cargo xtask validate --solver sparse`: **28/28**, no printed error figure differs from the dense
run, with the transient gates now integrated sparse throughout. `bench-scale`, two runs: a whole
`.tran` at 802 unknowns (101 points, now sparse under `Auto`) **68–236 ms**, 0.68–2.3 ms per
point, against 3.0–3.5 s (29.7–35.0 ms per point) dense in the 1.6.0 runs earlier the same day.
The second run was noisy throughout (its dense `.ac` column doubled too), hence the range.


1.6.0 - 2026-09-23
------------------
**Sparse solve, Step 2: DC uses it from 500 unknowns.** `NewtonConfig` gains `solver`
(`Solver::Auto` by default: dense below 500 unknowns, sparse from 500). The Newton loop keeps one
`SparseSystem` and one `SparseLu` for the whole solve, every `gmin` stage included, so the
pattern is found once and the symbolic factorization done once. Damping's trial points go into
a scratch sparse system. Below 500 unknowns the dense branch runs the same statements as before.

`va-cli sim --solver auto|dense|sparse` (default auto). `va-cli` gains `solve_dc_with`,
`solve_dc_sweep_with`, `solve_transient_with`, `solve_ac_with` and `solve_noise_with`, and
`va-harness` the matching `run_*_with`; the old names delegate with `Auto`, so no caller changes.
A third pre-flight line names the solver, why it was chosen, and what it covers. For `.tran`,
`.ac` and `.noise` that is only the starting operating point: their own loops stay dense until
Steps 3–4, and the line says so. `cargo xtask validate --solver sparse` runs every golden gate on
the sparse path; it also runs as a test, so CI keeps checking it.

Found while writing the tests: a ladder with a diode at every node driven at 2 V fails with
`Singular` on both paths alike (plain Newton overflows the junctions), so it says nothing about
the solver; the test drives it at 0.5 V.

Measured at 1.6.0: `cargo test --workspace` **862 passed / 0 failed** (+7: four Newton tests
comparing the paths through `gmin` stepping, damping, `Auto` above the threshold, and a floating
node with the rescue; the pre-flight line; `--solver sparse` on four decks including the 1.4.1
redundant-short decks; `validate` with the sparse solver forced). fmt + clippy clean.
`cargo xtask validate`: **28/28**, output identical line for line to 1.4.1.
`cargo xtask validate --solver sparse`: **28/28**, every printed error figure the same as the
dense run's. `bench-scale`, two runs: a whole `.op` at 802 unknowns (now sparse under `Auto`)
**2.0–2.3 ms**, against 63.46 ms dense on 2026-09-17; the dense 402-unknown row took 9.0–9.8 ms
in the same runs. The dense-calibrated cost estimate still prints for sparse runs and overstates
them; recalibrating it is Step 5.


1.5.0 - 2026-09-23
------------------
**Sparse solve, Step 1: built and tested, not used by any analysis yet.** New module
`va_core::sparse` (docs/proposals/sparse-solve.md): `Solver { Dense, Sparse, Auto }` with
`SPARSE_THRESHOLD = 500`; `SparseSystem`, a `StampSink` that stores the Jacobian and the charge
Jacobian over one shared compressed-column pattern and never allocates `dim²`; `assemble_into`;
and `SparseLu`, which caches the symbolic factorization per pattern so each Newton iteration
redoes only the numeric one. The pattern is found on the first assembly and grows to the union
if a model later stamps an entry it had not (an `if` arm or an `@(above)` body), with exactly
one new symbolic factorization. Entries stamped `0.0` and every diagonal are kept in the
pattern. No Interface β change: models only ever see `(row, col, value)` calls.

It keeps the dense path's guarantees: `faer`'s sparse-LU panic on some singular inputs is caught
and reported as `Singular`, the post-solve residual check uses the same tolerance, and
`NonFinite` names the same entry dense would (the first bad one in row-major order, though the
sparse values are stored column by column). The prototype `linsolve::solve_sparse` stays as the
benchmark reference.

Why a minor bump for code nothing calls yet: it is new public API and the proposal counts each of
Steps 1–4 as a feature. Nothing a user runs changes: dense stays the only path until Step 2.
Limitation: the slot lookup per stamp is a hash lookup; a faster per-instance cache is deferred
until a profile asks for it.

Measured at 1.5.0: `cargo test --workspace` **855 passed / 0 failed** (+13: 11 in
`va_core::sparse`, and `bench-linsolve`'s row test now also checks the Step 1 path against
dense). fmt + clippy clean. `xtask validate` not re-run: no analysis path changed.
`cargo xtask bench-linsolve` gained the Step 1 path; two release runs, ladder circuit: at 501
unknowns one solve takes 0.06 ms against dense's 5.7–7.2 ms, and one Newton iteration (assembly
+ solve) 0.12–0.14 ms against 6.6–10.1 ms; at 10 001, 5.1–5.4 ms against 8.2–10.1 s per
iteration. The ladder is tridiagonal, sparse LU's best case, and the benchmark times matrix work
only, so whole-run gains on real circuits will be smaller. Step 5 measures those.


1.4.1 - 2026-09-23
------------------
**Redundant ideal shorts no longer make the matrix singular.** `V(a,b) <+ 0.0` gives each
instance its own branch-current row saying `V(a) - V(b) = 0`. Two instances across the same
pair of nodes wrote that row twice, and the solve failed with `singular matrix`. This is how
the bug was first seen: two BSIM-BULK devices sharing one thermal node, whose self-heating-off
arm is `Temp(t) <+ 0.0`. `circuits/benchmark/nand/README.md` recorded it as a thermal-node bug.
It was not one: twelve lines with no compact model reproduce it.

`va_codegen::lower::ideal_shorts` proves, per compiled model and under its parameter values,
which branches are ideal shorts. Only what can be proven counts: every potential contribution
that can run folds to zero from literals and parameters, no flow contribution can run (for
BSIM-BULK's mixed `if (SHMOD...) Pwr(t) <+ ... else Temp(t) <+ 0.0`, the condition folds from
parameters), and the branch current is never read. `va-cli` runs a union-find over every short
in deck order (`ShortForest`). A short whose ends are already held equal is pinned to zero
current through the new `CompiledModel::instantiate_with_shorts`. That covers duplicates,
reversed duplicates, a short from a node to itself, and loops of shorts. Node voltages and
every other current are unchanged. The pinned instance reports `0` on its own auxiliary row
(`X2.b0`) and the kept one reports the total, which is the one visible difference.

Limitations: a mixed branch whose arm depends on a variable, even one computed from parameters
alone, is not proven to be a short and is left alone. Loops through deck voltage sources or a
nonzero `V(p,n) <+ expr` are still singular, as in any MNA simulator.

Measured at 1.4.1: `cargo test --workspace` **842 passed / 0 failed** (+3: redundant shorts,
a loop of shorts, and which branches qualify); with pinning disabled the two deck tests fail
with the original `singular matrix`. `cargo xtask validate` **28 checked, 0 failed, 28/28**,
output identical line for line to 1.4.0+2 (checked by stashing the change and diffing, apart
from cargo's build lines). fmt + clippy clean. The NAND decks `bsimbulk107`, `bsimcmg`,
`bsimimg` and `lutsoi` with their four thermal nodes merged into one: all solve, and `V(out)`
matches the separate-node decks to 1e-16 V over the whole sweep. The decks themselves keep
separate nodes; only their header comments changed.


1.4.0+2 - 2026-09-23
--------------------
**Docs only: a proposal for a sparse linear solve** (`docs/proposals/sparse-solve.md`). No code
changes. Dense LU stays for circuits below 500 unknowns, as it is today; from 500 unknowns the
solve would be sparse (`faer`'s pure-Rust sparse LU), with a `--solver dense|sparse|auto`
override. 500 rather than a higher value because `.tran` is already impractical on dense LU
between ~800 and ~1 600 unknowns (docs/validation.md, 2026-09-17), and because a threshold every
golden gate sits under leaves the sparse path untested unless it can be forced; the proposal
runs all 28 gates with `--solver sparse` as well. Five steps, each its own release: sink + solve,
DC, transient, AC/noise, then measuring the real crossover, which may move the 500.

Also corrects `docs/future_development.md` §1, which said sparse assembly needs an Interface β
change. It does not: `StampSink` only receives `(row, col, value)` calls, so a sparse sink is a
new implementation in `va-core` and no model or trait changes.

Nothing was run for this entry beyond `cargo metadata` for the version; the figures quoted in
the proposal come from the 2026-08-31 and 2026-09-17 measurements, which it cites by date.


1.4.0 - 2026-09-22  (tagged v1.4.0)
-----------------------------------
**A release, and a correction to how the last two were numbered.** Nothing in the code changes
here. 1.3.1 (`gmin` stepping wired in) and 1.3.2 (the elaborated IR shared across instances)
are both features, and the rule at the top of this file says a feature after 1.0 bumps the
*minor*, not the patch. They bumped the patch. Rather than rewrite two published entries, this
entry is where the minor moves, and the tag is cut here.

What `v1.4.0` carries over `v1.0.0`, the previous tag, newest first:

  * **The elaborated IR is shared across instances of one model** (1.3.2). An 80-device PSP103
    inverter chain went 413 MB / 14.1 s -> 78 MB / 9.1 s; the per-device memory slope fell
    5.1 MB -> 0.78 MB, and what is left is the dense Jacobian rather than the models.
  * **`gmin` stepping, as a rescue** (1.3.1): tried only after a plain solve fails, so
    converging circuits stay bit-identical. The NAND decks went 6 -> 11 of 12 solving.
  * **`$table_model`** (1.3.0): a user can bring their own measured data, one `x y` pair per
    line, with per-end extrapolation control. Array data sources and dimensions beyond the
    first are structured refusals, not silent approximations.
  * **A transient starts from the operating point unless the deck says UIC** (1.3.0 as
    originally numbered, see 1.2.5+1), matching SPICE.
  * **DC sweeps build their instances once and continue from the previous point** (1.2.1,
    1.2.2): a 1001-point BSIM4 sweep went 30.0 s -> 0.63 s.
  * **The evaluator stops paying for work it does not do** (1.2.0): a sparse-constant gradient
    representation plus a hoisted bias-independent setup, 3.4-9.3x on the CMC standard models.
  * **Two `0*inf` NaNs in the AD rules** (1.1.1), found by the CMC standard models and fixed —
    BSIM4, PSP103 and BSIM-BULK could not solve before it.
  * **Every `sim` says its size and a rough cost before it solves** (1.1.0).

Known open, and not fixed by this release: two instances of a model that clamps a shared node
to zero (`Temp(t) <+ 0.0`, what a compact model writes when self-heating is off) give a
singular matrix, because each instance contributes its own identical constraint row for the
same ideal short. Diagnosed this version and reproducible in twelve lines; the five-port decks
in `circuits/benchmark/nand/` work around it by giving each instance its own thermal node.
See that directory's README §2.

Measured at 1.4.0: `cargo test --workspace` 839 passed / 0 failed; `cargo xtask validate`
28 checked, 0 failed, convergence 28/28; `cargo fmt` and `cargo clippy --workspace
--all-targets -- -D warnings` clean.

1.3.2+1 - 2026-09-22
--------------------
**Two `va-core` tests pinned the *variant* a deliberately-unsolvable fixture fails with, and
macOS CI went red on it.** Both used the 20-diode-chain-at-20 V fixture and asserted
`Err(CoreError::Singular)`; the property either test actually needs is only that plain Newton
fails, so that `gmin` succeeding afterwards proves something.

Which way that chain comes apart is budget-dependent, and therefore platform-dependent.
Measured here at this version:

    max_iters=  20 -> NoConvergence { residual: 3.47e23 }
    max_iters=  50 -> Singular
    max_iters= 100 -> Singular      <- NewtonConfig::default(), what dc.rs used

Fifty iterations of margin. A platform whose `exp` and FMA contraction take a few more
iterations to walk a node into the overflow range lands on `NoConvergence` at the default
budget instead — which is a portability bug in the assertion, not a difference in the solver.
`newton.rs`'s sibling ran at `max_iters: 2000`, well clear of the boundary, and is loosened
here too as the same defect rather than because it was observed failing.

`dc.rs` now asserts the precondition the rescue actually has — the error is one
`worth_a_gmin_retry` accepts — and `newton.rs` asserts `is_err()`. The observed variant stays
in both comments, as an observation rather than a contract. No non-test code changed.

Stated plainly: the macOS failure was diagnosed from the code and reproduced only by the
budget sweep above, not from the CI log (no `gh` on this machine), so the confirmation that
this is the failing assertion is the next green macOS run.

Measured at this version: `cargo test -p va-core` 40 passed / 0 failed; `cargo clippy
--workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings` and `cargo fmt` clean.

1.3.2 - 2026-09-22
------------------
**The elaborated IR is shared across instances of the same model.** `build_instance` cloned the
whole `va_ir::Module` per device and lowered it again, so a deck paid for the arena once per
*device* rather than once per *model card*. For PSP103 that arena is large: a chain of 40
CMOS inverters (80 devices, two cards) peaked at **413 MB** and took **14.1 s**; the same deck
now peaks at **78 MB** in **9.1 s**. Per device the memory slope falls from 5.1 MB to 0.78 MB,
and what is left is the dense Jacobian, not the IR:

    devices     10      20      40      80
    before    56.5   103.4   199.4   413.2  MB
    after     23.3    25.5    36.4    78.1  MB

`va_codegen::GeneratedModel` is now an `Rc<SharedModel>` (module, resolved parameters, lowered
plan, junction classification, evaluated setup) plus the one thing that is genuinely per
instance: the local-slot -> global-unknown map. The new `CompiledModel::{new, instantiate}`
exposes the split; `build_instance` is unchanged and now calls both, so nothing outside
`va-cli` had to move. `va-ir` and `va-abi` are untouched — this is not an Interface alpha/beta
change.

**What the sharing is keyed on, and why that is the whole correctness question.** `va-cli`
caches compiled models per `(module, positional value, named overrides, unconnected ports)`.
The first three are obvious in hindsight — parameter values are baked into the compiled model,
so a PMOS written `TYPE=-1` is not the NMOS written `TYPE=1`. The fourth was not: marking a
port unconnected *mutates* the module, and `$port_connected` changes what the elaborated code
does, so two devices differing only in whether they wire an optional terminal need two
compiled models. That analysis therefore had to move ahead of the compile.

Each of the three keyed properties was checked by deliberately dropping it and confirming a
gate turns red, not by assuming: `two_devices_from_one_model_keep_their_own_parameters` (new)
reports 0.5 V instead of 0.75 V when either parameter half is dropped, and the existing
`fiber_mzi_noise_matches_wanser_and_duan_through_the_interferometer` fails when port
connectivity is dropped — `circuits/fiber_mzi_noise.net` places two waveguides with identical
overrides, one with its PZT control port wired and one without.

The cost, stated: models with *different* parameters still compile separately, which is
correct rather than optimised away, so a deck of N individually-tuned devices sees none of
this. The sweep rebuild path also keeps a per-point cache, since the swept value is part of
the key and nothing could be shared across points anyway.

Measured at this version: `cargo test --workspace` **839 passed / 0 failed** (838 + the new
key test); `cargo xtask validate` **28 checked, 0 failed, convergence 28/28**; corpus
`check external --recursive` 69/73 self-contained, 86 passes — all unchanged. `cargo fmt` and
`cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings` clean.

1.3.1 - 2026-09-22
------------------
**`gmin` stepping is wired in — as a rescue, not as a routine.** `va_core::convergence` has had
the homotopy since T3.3 and `NewtonConfig::gmin_steps` defaulted to **0**, so it never ran. A
node with no DC path was therefore simply fatal, and that is the ordinary shape of a series
stack: in a CMOS NAND the node between the two pull-down devices floats whenever the lower one
is off. Six of the twelve decks in `circuits/benchmark/nand/` could not be solved at all.

  **Eleven of twelve now solve; eight are clean NANDs.** BSIM-CMG and L-UTSOI became clean
  (1.200000 V -> 3.70e-8 V and 1.41e-10 V); BSIM-SOI and EKV2.6 now solve but pull down only to
  0.437 V and 1.099 V, which at default parameters is the device rather than the solver. HiSIM2
  still fails for its own reasons, and BSIM-IMG's deck is wrong rather than its solve.

  **The plain solve is tried first, always.** Only a failure the homotopy can plausibly rescue
  — `Singular`, `NoConvergence` or `NonFinite`, each observed on a real deck — earns a second
  attempt with the ladder, and a caller that already asked for stepping is left alone. That
  ordering is the design, not an optimisation: the ladder is `gmin_steps + 1` full Newton
  solves, so running it unconditionally would multiply the cost of every DC point in every
  circuit, including the large majority that converge on the first try and need none of it.
  As a fallback those circuits pay **nothing** and their answers stay bit-identical.

  The evidence for that last claim is the gate: `cargo xtask validate` is 28/28 with all 28
  error figures **identical digit for digit** to the run before the change. A test states it
  directly too, comparing a converging circuit's solution through `dc::operating_point` against
  `newton::solve` with `==` rather than a tolerance.

  **Both DC entry points share one rescue.** `dc::operating_point` gets it as well as
  `operating_point_continued`, because `.ac` and `.noise` linearize around the former — without
  that, a circuit with a floating node would solve under `.op`/`.dc` and fail under `.ac`, the
  same circuit with a different verdict.

  **The ladder's size was measured, not chosen.** 30 stages at >=150 iterations each: 10, 20, 30
  and 40 stages all still failed the 20-diode chain at the default 100-iteration budget, so the
  binding constraint was the per-stage budget rather than the ramp. The final unshunted stage
  has to walk from the last shunted solution to the real answer, and on a long series chain that
  is more than 100 steps. Only failed solves ever pay for it.

  **If the rescue also fails, the *original* error surfaces.** It describes the real circuit and
  names the row that went singular or non-finite; the laddered one describes a shunted variant
  the user never wrote.

  Measured at this version. `cargo test --workspace` 838 passed / 0 failed (2 new). `cargo xtask
  validate` 28 checked, 0 failed golden, convergence 28/28, every figure identical. Corpus
  `va-cli check external --codegen` 200/224.

  **Not fixed, and still the next thing in that folder:** two instances of a five-port model
  sharing one thermal node give a singular matrix, reproduced in four lines in
  `circuits/benchmark/nand/README.md` §2. `gmin` does not rescue it — it shunts `Node` rows, and
  whatever is degenerate there is not one. Root cause still not established.

1.3.0 - 2026-09-22
------------------
**`$table_model` — a user can bring their own data.** LRM §9.21, one dimension, file source.
The first answer to "could we let users add their own `$` functions": the LRM already has one,
and it covers what people usually mean by it — measured data, a lookup — without opening a hole
in the AD contract that a black-box function would.

    I(p, n) <+ V(p, n) / $table_model(V(t), "thermistor.tbl", "1CL");

  **The file is read at elaboration, not at evaluation**, and that is the load-bearing choice.
  It is what the LRM describes (§9.21.1: the data source's state "is captured on the first
  call") and what keeps `ModelInstance::load` a pure function of `(x, ctx, committed state)`. A
  lookup that touched the filesystem per evaluation would be re-run by every Newton iteration
  and by every rejected timestep, and would break both. Only the numbers reach the IR — no path,
  no handle, no reader — so Interface β is untouched.

  The table rides **flattened into the call's own arguments**, the same trade `noise_table` made
  in 2026-08-04: an `Expr` variant carrying a `Vec<(f64, f64)>` would have to be taught to every
  arena walk, clone, const-folder and pretty-printer, while `Const` arguments to an ordinary
  `Call` are already handled by all of them. Interface α change, recorded in
  `docs/interfaces.md` with the argument layout and the packed control code.

  **The derivative is the point.** A lookup whose Jacobian did not match its value would not
  error — it would converge Newton to the wrong answer, the failure mode three separate bugs
  took this week. So the segment slope is returned with the value and finite-difference gated
  per §5 (`table_model_derivative_matches_finite_difference`). At a knot the function has a
  kink and no derivative exists; the right-hand segment is taken, the same subgradient
  convention `abs` uses at zero, and that is written down rather than left to be discovered.

  **Defaults are the LRM's, which are not the obvious ones:** linear interpolation and *linear*
  extrapolation at both ends, not constant. A model that wants its table clamped has to say so.

  **Refused, not approximated** — each a `Refusal` naming what to write instead: a second lookup
  expression (N-D), the array data source, `2`/`3` spline interpolation, `I` ignore-column, `E`
  error-extrapolation, and the `;N` dependent-column selector. A missing or malformed file stays
  a plain elaboration error, because that is a broken input rather than a declined construct, and
  §5 makes the distinction the first thing a user debugging a run needs. Notably `E` cannot be
  honoured at all: it makes an out-of-range lookup a fatal error, and `load` may not fail.

  **Template example**, the second half of the request: `models/thermistor.va` +
  `models/thermistor.tbl` + `circuits/thermistor_rt.net`. An NTC thermistor whose resistance is
  a measured R(T) table rather than a formula, with the table driven by a *node voltage* so it
  participates in the circuit equations rather than being read once at setup. Verified against
  the table's own rows: exact at every knot, 5630.0000 Ω interpolated at 295.15 K against a
  hand-computed 5630.0000, constant below the range and linear above it. The model's own comment
  works out where `"L"` at the top end reaches zero resistance (≈398 K, 25 K past the data) —
  because a control string is a judgement and the example should say what it costs.

  Also: `elaborate_unit` now takes the compilation unit's include path, so a data file resolves
  exactly as an `` `include `` does. The older entry points pass an empty path and keep working.

  Measured at this version. `cargo test --workspace` 836 passed / 0 failed (4 new AD tests).
  `cargo xtask validate` 28 checked, 0 failed golden, convergence 28/28. Corpus `va-cli check
  external --codegen` 200/224, 182/191 self-contained.

  **On the version.** Published first as `1.2.6` and relabelled **1.3.0** the same day: §12 has a
  feature bump the minor after 1.0, and `$table_model` is a feature rather than one of the 1.2
  fixes the line was being held open for. The 1.2 line closes at 1.2.5+4.

  What cannot move is the commit subject of a6e23b0, which still reads `1.2.6` — rewriting a
  pushed commit would mean a force-push. Same reconciliation as 1.2.5+1's, in the other
  direction: `git log` says 1.2.6 for that commit and every file says 1.3.0.

1.2.5+4 - 2026-09-22
--------------------
**The PSP103 inverter, with its real model card.** `external/benchmarkExt/` supplied the
`Modelcards/psp103_{nmos,pmos}.mod` that had been missing — 283 lines each, of which **269
parameters per device map onto PSP103VA and nothing fails to match** (`level` is the
simulator's dispatch, not a parameter). `circuits/benchmark/psp103_inverter_card_{vtc,tran}.net`
carry them, folded onto the device lines because this parser has no `.model` card and no `+`
continuation; SPICE keys are case-insensitive and Verilog-A parameters are not, so they were
matched case-insensitively against the 880 the module declares.

  Best of three, against the same deck's reported OpenVAF time:

      .tran 10p 10n, 1810 accepted points     3.99 s
      .dc V1 0 1.2 0.012, 101 points          0.45 s
      both, as the original card runs them    4.44 s      OpenVAF 20.1 s

  **This closes the caveat that mattered.** The worry with the previous figures was that `.va`
  defaults made an easier device, so the gap might have been a step-count gap rather than a
  speed one. It was not: **1810 accepted timepoints with the reference card against 1861 with
  the defaults.** Both runs do the same amount of integration; the circuit, the parameters and
  the work are now the same. What is still not the same is the hardware — the OpenVAF figure was
  supplied rather than measured here, and its 20.1 s includes whatever ngspice spends around
  the solve.

  The card moves the device where it should, which is its own evidence that the 269 parameters
  landed: the transfer curve switches at Vin ~ 0.59 V against a 0.6 V rail-splitting ideal,
  peak supply current rises 98.9 uA -> **190.4 uA**, leakage 20.7 pA -> **10.3 nA**, and
  small-signal gain drops 69 V/V -> **10.3 V/V**. A real 0.1 um device rather than a default
  one. Rails 1.199991 V and 4.42e-6 V.

  Both parameter sets are kept, and deliberately: the difference between the two decks is
  exactly what a model card is worth. Plots for each, in `docs/examples/`.

  **Still out of reach, and worth naming:** the same drop contains
  `iscas85_benchmark_circuit/` — 89,240 lines, 7,119 subcircuit instances. It needs `.subckt`,
  which this parser does not have, and it would put tens of thousands of unknowns through a
  dense LU. Those are `benchmark.md` § 7's first two open items, now with a concrete deck
  attached to them.

  Decks, plots and docs only, hence a build digit; no code changed. v1.2.5's gate figures stand:
  832 tests, `cargo xtask validate` 28/28.

1.2.5+3 - 2026-09-22
--------------------
**A NAND2 per MOSFET model**, in `circuits/benchmark/nand/`: thirteen decks, twelve of them the
same CMOS gate built from a different vendor model, plus one using this project's own
`models/mosfet.va` that needs nothing from `external/`.

  The point of a NAND rather than another inverter is the series stack. Its internal node
  `N001` floats whenever the lower device is off, which a single transistor never exercises, and
  that is where nearly every result came from.

  **Six work as shipped** — BSIM4, BSIM6, BSIM-BULK 107, PSP102, PSP103, PSP104 — all with a
  clean rail-to-rail swing at Vdd = 1.2 V on default parameters. The other six split into four
  distinct causes, listed in `nand/README.md` with the numbers:

  * **`gmin` is implemented and never runs.** `va_core::convergence` has gmin stepping and
    `NewtonConfig::default()` sets `gmin_steps: 0`, so the homotopy never happens and a floating
    node is simply fatal. Measured by temporarily setting it to 10: BSIM-CMG, BSIM-SOI, L-UTSOI
    and EKV2.6 all stop failing — **6 of 12 becomes 10 of 12**. L-UTSOI becomes a clean NAND
    (1.200000 V -> 1.41e-10 V); BSIM-SOI and EKV2.6 solve but pull down only to 0.437 V and
    1.099 V, which at default parameters is plausibly the device rather than the solver. This is
    `CLAUDE.md` §3's own backlog item for `va-core`, and it is now costing real circuits.
  * **Two instances sharing a thermal node give a singular matrix — a bug**, reproduced in four
    lines and not about the NAND at all. One instance on that node solves; two on *separate*
    nodes solve; two sharing one node does not. Four-port models sharing a node are fine
    (`psp103.net` has M3 and M4 sharing `vdd` as their bulk), so it is specific to the extra
    port. Root cause not established. The five-port decks work around it with a node per
    instance, marked as a workaround in each header.
  * **BSIM-IMG's deck is wrong, not the simulator.** It is an independent-double-gate device —
    ports `(d, fg, s, bg, t)`, front and *back* gate — and mapping the NAND's bulk onto `bg`
    drives the back gate as if it were a body contact. The degraded 0.79 V swing is that mistake
    faithfully simulated. Fixing it means choosing a back-gate bias, which is a device decision.
  * **HiSIM2 fails for its own reasons**, non-finite at an internal node of a PMOS with gmin on
    or off. Not diagnosed.

  `zoo_nmos.net` reproduces the floating-node failure in eight lines with no external model, and
  carries a 1 GOhm resistor standing in for the gmin that is not there — with its header saying
  so, rather than shipping the workaround quietly.

  Decks and docs only, hence a build digit: **no code changed**, and the `gmin_steps: 10` used to
  measure the second bullet was reverted before anything was written. v1.2.5's gate figures
  stand: 832 tests, `cargo xtask validate` 28/28.

1.2.5+2 - 2026-09-22

**Plots for the PSP103 inverter**, in `docs/examples/` beside the rest: `psp103_inverter_vtc.svg`
(V(out) against Vin), `psp103_inverter_crowbar.svg` (the supply current `I(Vmeas)` over the same
sweep, which is what the original deck plots), and `psp103_inverter_tran.svg` (V(in) and V(out)
over the full 10 ns, five cycles of the 2 ns clock).

  `benchmark.md` gains the inverter's wall time and `circuits/benchmark/README.md` points each
  deck at its plot. Measured, best of three: **4.78 s** for the 1861-point transient, **0.41 s**
  for the 101-point sweep, **5.19 s** for both as the original card runs them, against **20.1 s**
  reported for the same deck under OpenVAF.

  **That comparison is not like-for-like and the entry says so where it is recorded.** The
  OpenVAF figure was supplied rather than measured here, on unknown hardware — and the model card
  (`Modelcards/psp103_*.mod`) was never provided, so everything here runs on the `.va` file's
  defaults. Different parameters are a different device: different stiffness, different accepted
  timepoints, different work. Same deck, not the same computation.

  Figures a reader can check the plots against: rails at 1.200000 V and 7.49e-9 V, steepest
  transfer slope 69.1 V/V near Vin = 0.576 V, supply current peaking at 98.9 uA at Vin = 0.588 V
  and falling to 20.7 pA at both rails, Miller undershoot of -2.6 mV on the falling input.

  Plots and docs only, hence a build digit; no code changed and nothing was re-measured beyond
  the timings above. v1.2.5's gate figures stand: 832 tests, `cargo xtask validate` 28/28.

1.2.5+1 - 2026-09-22

**`1.3.0` is relabelled `1.2.4`.** It was published earlier today as a minor bump, but the
transient operating-point start belongs in the 1.2 line with the rest of this week's fixes, and
leaving it as published made this file read 1.2.5 above 1.3.0 above 1.2.3 — a sequence that goes
backwards in the middle.

  The label moved everywhere it appears: the entry heading here, two `docs/roadmap.md` entries,
  `benchmark.md`'s closed list, `circuits/benchmark/psp103_inverter_tran.net`'s header, and six

  **What could not move: the commit subject of 58f1388 still reads `1.3.0`.** Changing it means
